1318. WILLIAM FISHER , was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 guard-chain, value 2 l.; the goods of John Troup, his master.
WILLIAM WILLEY . I am in the service of Mr. Wood, pawnbroker, St. John-street, Clerkenwell. This chain was handed to me by the prisoner, about one o'clock, on the 24th of March—he asked me if it was gold—I said it was—he said he picked it up in the kennel, in Compton-street, that he dropped some halfpence, and he picked the chain up with them—I detained him.
JOHN TROUP . I am a wholesale jeweller, and live in Hatton-garden. The prisoner was my errand-boy on the 23rd of March—this chain is gold, and is mine—I had seen it safe on the evening of the 23rd of March—it was kept in the counting-house, but that evening I had used more aquafortis on it than I wished, and I put it in water to counteract it, and put it in my parlour—the prisoner left me about ten o'clock that night, and returned in the morning—I am sure it is my chain—a part of my label was on it when I saw it at the pawnbroker's—it is worth 40 s.—I think the servant took it away in the water, and threw it in the dust; and the next morning the prisoner was set to look in the dust for a ring that I had lost, and I think he found this chain there—I asked if he had seen it—he said he had not—that was between nine and eleven o'clock, and he did not leave my house till half-past twelve—he was rather later than usual.
HENRY HAYWARD ( police-constable G 97.) The prisoner was given into my charge—he said he found the chain in the kennel—he said he lived at Mr. Troop's, but he gave a different number.
Prisoner's Defence. My master sent me to the dust-hole to look fora ring, and I found the chain; I kept it to see if he said anything about it, and he did not; I took it to the shop to see if it was gold; he asked me about it before I looked into the dust.
GUILTY . Aged 13.— Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18430403-1319]
1319. WILLIAM JEFFRIES , and JOHN BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of March, 1 half-crown, the monies of Francis William Allen.
HARRIET ALLEN . I am the wife of Francis William Allen, who keeps the City Arms public-house at Ratcliffe. On the afternoon of the 9th of March Jeffries was in my house, and had some gin—I saw him look to the bar-door, and beckon to Brown to come in, which he did—I believe they drank the gin together—Jeffries then tendered a shilling to pay for the gin—my little girl was going to give him change, and he said, "Nevermind, give me half-a-crown for two shillings and sixpenny worth of halfpence"—she said to me, "Can you, mother?"—I said, "Yes," I took a half-crown, put it on the counter, and put my hand on it—when I took my hand off to look at a counterfeit shilling which he offered, he took the half-crown up—he was in the act of counting the halfpence, when I observed to my little girl that one of the shillings which he had put down in change for the good half-crown was a counterfeit—Jeffries put the half-crown into his waistcoat pocket, and he would not give it up—he had put down two shillings, one of which was a very bad one, and Brown took up the good shilling, the counterfeit one, and the halfpence—I called the pot-boy to get a constable—my husband, who was very ill, closed
the door when he heard the prisoners were going—I asked Jeffries to give me my half-crown, and he denied having it—it was taken without giving me any change.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You put down your half-crown?
A. Yes—it was a good one, and it was taken up by Jeffries—I saw two shillings put down by him—he had not put all the halfpence down—when I asked Jeffries for the half-crown he denied that he had taken it up—he denied it before Brown took up the two shillings—I said, "You have it in your waistcoat pocket"—Brown beard that—Jeffries said, "I don't know what you mean by half-a-crown"—he put his hand into his pocket, and said he had not got it—I saw Brown take up the two shillings and the halfpence, but I was so confused at the time, I do not know where he put them—Jeffries was not insisting on having his change—he had got my half-crown, and his change was lying on the counter—Brown said, "Is it a bad one? let me look," and took the shilling from the little girl's hand—he said to Jeffries, "You must have taken this from your brother; you must take this to your brother."
COURT. Q. Did they get the shillings back? A. Yes, both the good one and the bad one, and the halfpence—I saw Jeffries take up my good half-crown—Brown could see that—he was standing directly opposite me—the half-crown was full in his view—I should almost venture to say he could not help seeing it—it was exactly before his eyes—Jeffries denied having it, and then Brown swept up the change, and took it up—they did not pay for the gin—they were searched at the station—a mob collected round the door after they were put out—there was a mob round the tap-room window—the officer had hold of them in front of the window till the boy went for another officer—they were then taken through the crowd of twenty or thirty people.
Jeffries. Q. When I came in who served me? A. My little girl—I was in the bar parlour—I was at the bar parlour window—my daughter did not come back to me with the shilling—you took up my half-crown.
EMMA ALLEN . I live at the City Arms. On the 9th of March I saw both the prisoners there—they had half-a-quartern of gin—Jeffries put down a shilling to pay for it—I was going to take it up, and he said, "Stop a minute; will you give me half-a-crown for two shillings and sixpenny-worth of half-pence?"—I asked my mother—she said, "Yes"—she took out half-a-crown, put it on the counter, and Jeffries threw down another shilling—I took up that shilling, and said, "Mother, this is a rank bad one"—Brown said, "Is it a bad one?"—I said, "Yes, it is nothing but a piece of lead"—he then took up the other shilling and the halfpence, and Jeffries took the half-crown—Brown said, "This you must have taken of your brother; you must go back and change it"—Brown put the bad shilling, the other shilling, and the halfpence, into his pocket—my mother told Jeffries he had put the half-crown into his waistcoat pocket, and he denied it—we called the pot-man out of the parlour, and told him to fetch the policeman—the policeman said if we got them outside he would take them in charge—there was a crowd outside—the prisoners passed through that crowd.
Jeffries. Q. Did you not go to your mother with the shilling? A. No—she was in the bar.
Cross-examined. Q. When Brown took the bad shilling from you to look at, and took up the other money, had Jeffries taken up the half-crown? A. He took it up at the same time—I am sure I saw him take it up—he was counting the halfpence, and had put down the two shillings—when Brown took up the other money, Jeffries had taken the half-crown—my mother asked Jeffries for the half-crown, and he denied having it.
TIMOTHY HERLEHY ( police-constable K 329.) Between six and seven o'clock in the evening I was called to the City Arms—at first I mistook the nature of the charge, and begged the prisoners might be put outside before I took them—Mrs. Allen charged them with stealing the half-crown—Jeffries said he had not got it—I had a difficulty in keeping them till the other officer came—I was outside the door, holding them for nine or ten minutes—Jeffries struggled more than Brown—Brown had an opportunity of getting rid of the money—I found seven good sixpences on Brown, and 1 s. 6 d. in copper, and on Jeffries a penny-piece—Jeffries charged me with taking the half-crown from him.
Jeffries. I said if I had had the half-crown from the lady, he must have taken it; we were in custody all the time, and had no opportunity of getting rid of the money; if we had been struggling with this man, it is very evident he could not have held us. Witness, Jeffries struggled to get away, and said I had no authority to keep him; he charged me directly with having taken the half-crown from him; the sergeant and two or three more constables were in the station at the time.
WILLIAM SPEARY ( police-constable K 153.) I came up after Herlehy got to the house—Jeffries said to me, "Ah, old fellow, you are come too late, the half-crown is gone."
Jeffries. It is quite erroneous, I did not have the half-crown, it was a penny-piece I picked up off the counter.
JEFFRIES,— GUILTY . Aged 37.
BROWN,— GUILTY . Aged 26.
(There was another indictment against the prisoners.
[trialAccount t18430403-1320]
1320. WILLIAM DIX , was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 1 ring, value 1 d.; 3 half-crowns, 1 shilling, 2 sixpences, 1 penny, and 3 half-pence, the property of Joseph Allen, from his person.
JAMES TAYLOR . I live in Smith's-gardens, City-road, and am a constable of the Regent's-canal. On the morning of the 20th of March, I was on duty at No. 27 wharf—I saw the prisoner with the prosecutor Allen, who is captain of a boat called the Noah's Ark, which was on the basin—Allen was drunk, and the prisoner appeared to have been drinking a little—I saw them both on board the captain's boat, and I saw the prisoner shut the cabin-door, by pulling the slide—I considered he had gone to bed—about half-past two I was on the same wharf, and heard footsteps—I saw the prisoner with a bundle—I asked what he had got in that bundle—he said, "A piece of beef "—I asked how he came by it—he said, "The captain gave it me"—I took him to the boat—the captain was asleep on the side bed, and the cabin in a confused state—I told the prisoner I should take him to the station, and he ran away—I called, "Stop thief," and he was stopped by a policeman—I found on him three half-crowns, one shilling, two sixpences, one penny, five halfpence, this ring, and a tobacco-box—I asked what he had got, he said 17 s., and when I found this, he said it was his own money.
Prisoner. I said I thought about 17 s., as he gave it me when he was tipsy; I did not look at it; I kept him up till I could bring him on board, and then he gave me this bit of beef, and began to grumble at me; I came out and laid on a barge for about an hour, and then I got up and walked about. Witness. When I went down into the cabin I saw the captain lying on the side bed, and the cross-bed turned up; I found a dish of salt beef; the drawers and cupboards were open; the captain was so drunk I could not awake him.
JOSEPH ALLEN . I live in Staffordshire, and am captain of a trading boat, called the Noah's Ark; it was lying on the basin of the Regent's Canal—on
the 20th of March the prisoner helped me with my boat through the tunnel of the Regent's Canal—I then took him to a public-house and gave him some beer—I got drunk—I know I had got a ring and some money in my pocket, but I do not know how much—I did not count it—when I awoke I put my hand to my pocket, and missed my ring, and all my money, but one farthing-I also missed a piece of beef—I did not give him any beef, I am sure, nor did I tell him to take care of my money or ring—I am sure I gave him nothing but 6 d. for his work, and some bread and cheese—I did not take my ring out of my pocket, and tell him to take care of it—I saw the state the cabin was in when I awoke in the morning—I did not know what to think about it—it was turned upside down—it is not usually so.
Prisoner. You went to the Windsor Castle, and paid for a quartern of gin, pulled out the change and the rest of the money; I pulled you away from two policemen, and when we sat down in the cabin you gave me the beef. Witness. I had the money and this ring in this purse, in my pocket; they left me the purse and one halfpenny—I am sure I had the money when I went into the boat; I never handed over my money to him to keep; I am sure this is my ring; I had some half-crowns and small silver; I did not count it.
GUILTY . Aged 29.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1321]
1321. JOSEPH CARTER , was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Feb., 5 sovereigns, 17 shillings, 3 pence, 4 halfpence, and 4 10 l. Bank-notes, the monies of John Roberts.—2nd COUNT, for stealing an order for the payment of 47 l. 17 s. 5 d.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN ROBERTS . I am a seaman, and was lately employed on board a ship called the Barrett, Jun. —I was discharged from her in China, and received a paper for my wages—I lost the paper and my clothes on my passage home—I arrived in England about ten weeks ago, in the Cleopatra, a merchant vessel—on my arrival I applied to Mr. Soames, the owner of the Barrett, Jun., for my wages, and they would not give them me on account of the loss of my paper—they desired me to get two securities, and an affidavit—I went to the Thames police to make an affidavit, and there I saw a man named Kelly—he touched me on my shoulder, and took me to a public-house—he took me that night to the prisoner, who is landlord of the Black Horse, at Cock-hill, Ratcliffe-highway—I went with the prisoner and Kelly before the Lord Mayor, to make an affidavit—I was then lodging at the Black Horse—Kelly was to have come with me as security to Mr. Soames, but he got to high words with Soames, and Soames would not take him—the prisoner showed me a paper which he had signed, with a Mr. Johnson, a person whom the prisoner had obtained to put his name to it as security—I went with that paper, and the prisoner to Mr. Soames's office, in Ratcliffe-highway, not far from the house where I was stopping, on the 18th of Feb., and Mr. Soames's clerk paid me a cheque—I signed my name on the cheque—I was going to take it up myself, and the prisoner took it up before me—he took it from the hand of Soame's clerk—I then went with the prisoner to Williams's, the bankers in the City, in a cab —he had the cheque, and when we got to the banker's door he jumped out of the cab directly, and said nothing to me—he went to the bank directly, and I stopped in the cab a bit—I did not know which was the bank—I thought it was a public-house, or something—after a while the cab -man said, "Why don't you go in and get your money?"—I then followed the prisoner in, and when I got in he was just coming out, and putting the notes in his pocket-book—he said to me "It is all right, my lad "—the cheque was for 47 l. 17 s. 5 d.—I had not given him permission to put in
his pocket-book or in his pocket at all—I had at that time been lodging at his house, and I had got in debt to him—I was there a fortnight at 14 s. a week, and to drink out, which would come to 1 l.—I had agreed to give him something for becoming my security—I was willing to give him 5 l. altogether—after he had got these notes into his own pocket-book we went to a public-house close by—he offered me 34 l., and 34 l. 10 s.—I thought he was gammoning me—he did not produce the money—I offered to settle it with him if he would give me 35 l., but he did not produce the money—he said, "Come, don't be hard, lad; come, take 34 l. 10 s., "but he did not produce the money at all—I had previous to that told him I was going by the railway to Liverpool—I had made up my mind to go, and he told the cab -man to drive up there, still keeping my money—when we got there we went to the boxes to ask the fare, but they were not going to start till four o'clock, and they would not take the money down—the prisoner was willing to pay my passage down—I had drunk at different public-houses, and when I got to the railway I was about half-and-half —we went to the Railway public-house, and had something to eat and to drink—it was a very cold day, and I went to the tap-room fire to warm my feet—the prisoner brought me a glass of grog hot, and said, "Here, lad, here is 2 l. for you; take that for your pocket money now, and I will settle with you directly"—I am quite sure he said that after we left the railway station—he then went away, and I sat making myself comfortable for half an hour, for him to come back—the last time I saw him he was standing at the bar, talking to the landlord—he left me in the tap-room, and I lost sight of him—I did not see him again till he was taken up, which was about a month after—I went down to the Black Horse that very night—I paid 10 s. to a cab to take us there—I could not catch the prisoner—I stopped at the Black Horse three days, waiting for him—I had not a farthing but the 2 l., and I was forced to pay the man's passage back in the cab who had gone as a witness—Kelly came and took charge of the Black Horse instead of the prisoner—I got something to eat and drink there—I have never got a farthing from the prisoner from that time to this—after I had charged him I was taken from my house to two men, and I signed a paper, and received 8 l. from them—I was very badly off at the time—I had no means of subsistence, all my money was gone—I had been out for four years making this 47 l.
COURT. Q. At what part were you? A. On the north coast of China, in a troop-ship, with the expedition.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. You had some difficulty in getting this certificate of yours answered by Soames? A. Yes, I applied to Kelly, and afterwards to the prisoner, to try to get my money—I often saw him about getting this money for me, and when the prisoner made the security out I went with him to get this money—I did not give the certificate to him; Kelly gave it him—we got to Mr. Soames about ten o'clock—all the clerks were in the office—I know the clerk by sight who gave me the cheque—there were two or three more clerks there—I did not give the prisoner the cheque to get cashed on my behalf—I receipted it myself—it was before me and the prisoner—I took it out of the hand of the clerk, who took it back after I signed it—he brought it back, and the prisoner took it up—I did not express any sort of dissent to his taking it—I thought it was all right—I did not like to make a noise in the office—I took him to be an honest man—I was to pay him 5 l. for lodging, drink, security and all—I had been drinking a great many glasses on the day I got this cheque—I cannot tell exactly how many—I was too much in my glory when I got my money—I do not mean that it produced
an excitement, but I had a great deal of trouble to get my money paid—I was disposed to believe I should not have got my money without the prisoner—I suppose I was to have paid Johnson something for being a joint security—I do not know whether he got any thing.
COURT. Q. Did you mean that the prisoner should take up the cheque?
A. No, I was going to take it up myself.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you intend to receive your money yourself, or that it should find its way into the prisoner's pocket? A. I was going to receive it myself.
ALFRED WALLEN . I am clerk to Mr. Soames, a ship owner. I produce this cheque for 47 l. 17 s. 5 d. on Messrs. Williams—I received it from there—I remember it being paid—the prisoner and the prosecutor were both present—they came together—I delivered the cheque—the prosecutor made his mark to it, and I witnessed it on the back—I then put it on a desk before the prosecutor—I believe the prisoner took it, but I cannot swear it.
COURT. Q. Did you deliver it out as payment to Roberts? A. Certainly; the memorandum on it is a voucher that he had it as payment.
WILLIAM RAWLINSON . I am a clerk to Williams and Co., bankers, Birchin-lane. I paid the money for this cheque, but I cannot say to whom—I paid four 10 l. notes for it, and the rest in money—there were some sovereign amongst it—the notes were from No. 25516 to 25519.
HENRY JOHN PARKE ( police-sergeant K 10.) On the 14th of March I was in Arbour-square. I saw the prisoner in Arbour-street—a complaint had been made by the prosecutor at the Thames-police, and I followed the prisoner—he then crossed the road and turned round and beckoned me—he said, "I intended to have given myself up before this, having seen something in the paper, but I have not been able, having been laid up with the rheumatic gout"—and on the way to the station he said, "To tell you the truth I lost three of the ten pound notes before I left the terminus at Euston-squtre, and a five pound note."
COURT. Q. He could see you were after him? A. Certainly; he walked from me and then beckoned to me—I have known him eight or nine years.
Cross-examined. Q. What do you know of him generally? A. I was surprised when I heard of this transaction—I have known him in three houses, and thought him a respectable man.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What sort of houses did be keep? A. The last I believe was not a very respectable house—he was in partnership with Mr. Kelly—I believe Kelly is arrested for debt, and is in Whitecross-street—I cannot tell whether the prisoner's house was a crimping establishment.
MR. WILDE. Q. Do you not know that Kelly has turned out the prisoner's family? A. Yes; and very ill-used his wife I believe.
COURT to JOHN ROBERTS. Q. When the prisoner jumped out of the cab did you intend to give him permission to receive the money? A. No; I meant to get it myself—I had not intended he should have it at all—he never mentioned about a bank to me at all—I thought it was a public-house—I never allowed him the possession of this cheque out of my company, with my consent.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY on the 2nd COUNT . Aged 36.— Confined One Year.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1322. JOHN COATSWORTH , was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of Jan., 1 painting and frame, value 5 l.; the goods of Charles Dowton Smith.
JAMES FREEMAN SMITH . I live in Park-lane, and act as secretary at the
Adelaide Gallery, Strand—I received a painting and frame for sale from Mr. Charles Dowton Smith—I know it was there in Jan.—an officer named Pocock gave me information—I then went to the picture-gallery, and missed the picture—I am sure it had not been sold, or I must have known it—the officer produced me this ticket, which had been on the picture—it had been ticketed before it was lost—it denotes the place of the picture in our catalogue.
CHARLES DOWTON SMITH . I am an artist—in August last I sent this picture to the Adelaide Gallery—I saw it at Mr. Perrin's shop in Holborn on the 4th of March—this is it—it is worth 5 l. or five guineas.
HENRY HALE . I am shopman to Mr. Perrin, of Holborn-hill—he deals in pictures—in Jan. last the prisoner brought me this picture—I had not seen him before—he asked 4 s. for it, which we gave him—we did not consider it worth more—it had this frame on it—I am sure we bought it of the prisoner, and I think it was at the end of Jan.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-constable F 81.) On the 31st of Jan. the prisoner was in custody, and I went to a room it Leg-alley, Long-alley, and found this ticket, which had been on this picture at the gallery—I got further information, and went to the police-court and took the prisoner—I received the picture from Hale.
Prisoner's Defence. The officer found this ticket in the bottom of a closet, in Leg-alley, where I lodged; and he came and took me on suspicion—I do not know where the Adelaide Gallery is—I am totally ignorant of the painting and the shopman who says I sold it him.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1323]
1323. GEORGE FORD , and JOHN MALONEY , were indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 whip, value 3 s.; the goods of Thomas Tourle.
EMMA HUGHES . I am nine years old—I know I must speak the truth—I live with my mother in Cromer-street—I saw Mr. Tourle's cart at our door—there was a horse in the cart, and my little sister was with it—I knew the two prisoners before—I saw Ford come to the cart, get up on the wheel, take the whip out and run away with it—Maloney was on the opposite side of the way—he ran after Ford—I am quite sure they are the boys—I went and told of it.
WILLIAM TOOMES ( police-constable E 150.) About ten minutes to nine on the night of the 23rd of March I saw the two prisoners standing in a corner in a court which was no thoroughfare—I asked what they were doing—they said, nothing, they wanted to go to Lucas-place—I said that was not the way to Lucas-place they knew—they then came out and I found the whip—I took Maloney—Ford turned back and I took him.
THOMAS TOURLE . This is my whip—I left it in my cart at a door that evening.
Ford. I never saw the whip—we went down the court, and the policeman came.
Maloney. He asked who we wanted—I said a person named Knowlton, in Lucas-place—the policeman took us down a court and asked a woman to give him a whip.
WILLIAM TOOMES re-examined. When I first saw them the whip was concealed behind them—they were both close together—I did not see the whip in possession of either of them.
FORD,†— GUILTY . Aged 15.
MALONEY,†— GUILTY . Aged 16.
Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1324]
1324. GEORGIANA MATILDA BATE , was indicted for bigamy.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SIER ( police-constable G 29.) I took the prisoner into custody in Clerkenwell, on the 23rd of March—I produce an examined copy of the register of a marriage at St. Pancras-church, in 1834, between Georgiana Matilda Cartwright, and John Henry Bate—I examined it myself.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. What did you examine this with?
A. The clergyman read the book, and I held this paper in my hand, and then I looked the book over myself—I stood and looked at it, as he copied it from the register—I then read this over as he read the other—I then looked over the original, and compared it with this, word for word—I will swear this corresponds with the register.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did you go a second time, and examine the copy of a register at Islington-church? A. Yes—this purports to be a certificate of a marriage between Robert Carfrae and Georgiana Matilda Bate, on the 29th of Nov., 1840.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you do this? A. On Monday, the 27th—the name is spelt Carfy—I would not swear whether the name is Carfy or Carfye—this is a correct copy of the original—the prosecutor, Mr. Bate, came to me at the station, and directed me to go and get these certificates—I was before the Magistrate twice—I saw Mr. Bate there—he had a female with him—the prisoner was bailed in her own recognizances.
JOSEPH TOTMAN . I live in Park-place, City-road. I was present at a marriage, between John Henry Bate and the prisoner, at St. Pancras-church, on the 25th of December, 1834—I witnessed the register—her name was then Georgiana Matilda Cartwright—the prisoner is the woman—Mr. Bate is now alive—they lived together about twelve months—I frequented their place after they were married.
Cross-examined. Q. But you have seen them since then? A. I have not seen her above once or twice—I was in this box, I think, in April, 1839—I saw Mrs. Bate here then, and Mr. Bate was convicted and sent to prison—I heard he went to America some time after—I cannot say how long he remained there—I have not seen him till within these few days—I cannot say whether he married Elizabeth Chapman—I have not been to his house—I cannot say where he lives, nor how many children he has by this female—I do not know anything of his second marriage—I visited him and the prisoner about twelve months after their marriage—I do not know how he treated her—I never saw him use a poker to her—I do not know of her taking in washing—I have heard of her applying to Cripplegate-work house for relief, but I do not know it—she has some imperfection in her speech which is very much against her getting her living—Mr. Bate stated before the Magistrate that he had been to America.
WILLIAM BURLEY . I am a shoemaker, and live in Rose-street, Old-street, St. Luke's. I was present at Islington church on the 29th of Nov., 1840, at the marriage of the prisoner with Robert Carfrae.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known her? A. Eight years—I knew Mr. Bate—I did not visit them—there used to be a few quarrels between them at times—I never saw the poker used—I do not know how she got her living—she used to come to me sometimes, and say she was doing washing—I heard she applied for parochial relief—I heard from her that Bate was dead, or I should not have witnessed this ceremony—I was not present at Bate's trial—I do not know of his being absent a long while after the trial.
MR. DOANE. Q. Do you know that the prisoner had been ten times in custody for violently assaulting her husband? A. Only from hearsay—I
believe she told me she had been four times in the Compter, for assaulting her husband.
GUILTY . Aged 28.—Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18430403-1325]
1325. JANE TEBBUTT , was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 2 sovereigns, the monies of Jane Russell, her mistress.—2nd Count, stating them to be the monies of John Holmes.
MARTHA HOLMES . I am the wife of John Holmes, a plumber, at Turnham-green; the prisoner was my mother's servant. On the 1st of April she brought me a veil which I had left at my mother's on the previous evening and said she was going to her grandmother's—I told her to call for a parcel as she came back—after she was gone I put two sovereigns in a paper, put that into a piece of net, and inclosed the whole in a large sheet of paper, I put a pin at each end, and a pin in the middle—when she came back I gave it her, and told her to go straight to her mistress with it, and take care of it as there was something in it of consequence.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. How far do you live from King-street, Hammersmith? A. About a mile—her grandmother lives opposite me—I have sent her with parcels before, but not with money in them—in Feb. she brought me a pair of table-spoons, but it appears she looked at them—she told her grandmother so—I had borrowed this 2 l. of my mother about three weeks before—I had been in communication with her every day—she did not know of these sovereigns coming.
JANE RUSSELL . I am the mother of Martha Holmes; I live at Hammersmith. I sent the prisoner to my daughter with a veil, and she came back in about two hours—she brought me this piece of paper, with two bits of lace in it, but no money—the paper was pinned up with three pins—it was not tied at all—she followed me into the dining-room, I spread it out on a table, and said, "Jane, look here"—she took one of the bits of net, shook it, and said, "Here is nothing," and there was nothing—she then said she must go away—she was to have gone in a week's time, but she then said she must go directly, as her father was very bad—she left in leas than half an hour.
Cross-examined. Q. Had she given you notice? A. I had given her notice—I did not tell her to bring a parcel back—I did not expect one—I had seen my daughter two days before—she had not seen me on the day before—I had not lent her any money—I did not expect any money, nor any parcel—my daughter does not live above a mile from me—she visits me once or twice a week—the prisoner told me to look at the parcel, because there was something of great consequence there.
THOMAS GEORGE BUCK . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner at her mother's house, in the Fulham-road—I told her it was for stealing the money—she told me she opened the parcel herself, but did not see any money.
MR. HORRY called
JANE CARPENTER . My husband is a market-gardener. The prisoner is my granddaughter—I never told Mrs. Holmes that she had opened a parcel, and she never did do so.
NOT GUILTY
[trialAccount t18430403-1326]
1326. GEORGE WILLIAMS , was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 190 yards of mouseline de laine, value 4 l. 10 s. 3 d., the goods of William Hardwick and another; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH ROBERT WADDILOVE . I am assistant to Mr. William Hardwick and
another, of High Holborn. On the 27th of March I was coming down from the warehouse on the first floor into the shop—the stairs are opposite the door—I saw a boy taking the delaines from the door, where they had been tied with a string—he went off, and I followed him down Gray's Inn-lane to Fox-court, where he dropped them—I overtook him in a passage on the left-hand side, in Fox-court—it was the prisoner—I had not lost sight of him at all, only while I was going out of the shop—I saw him when I got into the street, with the delaines, and did not lose sight of him—he asked what I was taking him for—I said, "For stealing the de laine dresses"—he said, "I have not stolen them, I found them by the side of the pump"—I got the dresses, and brought him back to the shop, and gave him in charge—these are the dresses—here are nineteen of them, and about ten yards in each—they are worth 4 l. 10 s. 3 d.—they are the property of Messrs. Hardwick and Ford.
COURT. Q. Where were they? A. In the lobby, at the entrance of the shop—they might be a yard inside the door—I saw a person moving them from the door—I cannot swear it was the prisoner, but he was his height and size, and that sort of person; and as soon as I got out, which was scarcely a minute, I saw the prisoner with the mousseline delaine, and did not lose sight of him—I ran out directly.
FRANCIS HANCOCK ( police-constable T 70.) I was called into the prosecutors' shop about five o'clock, on the 27th of March, and took the prisoner in charge—he said he did not steal them, but found them by the side of the pump.
PAUL JONES ( police-constable T 37.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.

1328. JOHN MARRIOTT , was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 coat, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of George Buckland.
GEORGE BUCKLAND . I am butler to Mr. Snow, of Saville-row. On the 1st of April I was in the pantry, and heard somebody in the hall—I went and saw the prisoner just leaving the hall, with my coat on his back—as he was
going up the area steps he pulled the coat off, and threw it down—I pursued after him, and he was stopped at the corner of Clifford-street—this is my coat.
JAMES M'LOCHLAN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner—this coat was given to me at the house in Saville-row.
Prisoner's Defence. My grandmother sent me to Dr. Moore, who lives next door to the prosecutor, and I went down there by mistake—this coat was lying on the step, the butler came out and said, "If you don't be off, I will lock you up"—I ran off, and two men stopped me at the end of the street—I never had the coat on my back.
GEORGE BUCKLAND . He had it on both his arms—it had before been hanging in the servants' hall.

1329. CHARLES SMITH , was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 36 pence, 186 halfpence, and 11 farthings, the monies of Fergus Ferguson M'Gregor.
JOHN WILLIAMS . I live in Brownlow-street, and am an undertaker—about half-past eight o'clock in the morning of the 1st of April I went into Mr. M'Gregor's shop, in Broad-street, St. Giles's, to ask for a loaf—the prisoner caught my eye as I turned round—I had asked for the loaf before I got thoroughly in—the prisoner said, "They will be here directly"—he appeared to me to be putting the drawer in with his hand—he then came round the counter, and called down the passage, " Jem, you are wanted"—he turned round and went out—as soon as he got out I imagined he had been committing a felony—I followed and apprehended him—I asked what he had got in his cap—he said something belonging to his father—I said he must come back with me—he said he would not—Mr. M'Gregor came—we took him back, turned out his cap on the counter, and these coppers came out.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I shut the till? A. I saw the action at if you were shutting it—you were inside the counter, and had your cap down.
FERGUS FERGUSON M'GREGOR . I am a baker, and live in Broad-street, St. Giles's—on the morning of the 1st of April I left my shop for a little while—when I came back I noticed the prisoner coming out, holding his cap in his hand, with something heavy in it—I took him back and emptied his cap on the counter, and there was the money stated in the indictment—I looked in my till immediately, and missed 16 s. or 18 s. in copper, which I had seen, about five minutes previously—he begged forgiveness.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear to this money by any particular mark? A. No I had such money in my till.
JOHN, ROSE . I am a policeman. I was called in—I saw this copper on the counter with the cap—the prisoner said it was not Mr. M'Gregor's.
Prisoner's Defence. It was mine—I am a costermonger, and was going to market—I went in there for a penny loaf—nobody was there, and I chucked a penny on the counter—it rolled off, and I went round to look for it—this man came in—I went out—he came and said, "What have you got?—I said, "Some halfpence"—I can assure you it is mine.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
NEW COURT.— Monday, April 10 th, 1843.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common-Seigeant.

1336. THOMAS SCOTT , was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
EMMA DUTTON . I am housekeeper at Messrs. Golsing and Sharp, bankers, Fleet-street. The prisoner was in the habit of bringing bread from Mr. Inglis's to the house—I generally paid him weekly—this is the book in which it was entered when brought—here is 5 s. 11 d., and 6 s. 10 d., and the prisoner's receipt in the book for these sums.
THOMAS INGLIS . I am baker, and live in New-street, Covent-garden; the prisoner has been eleven years in my employ. It was his duty to take out bread, receive money, and to enter the sums he received in this cash-book every evening, and deliver it up to me—here is his cash-book—there is no entry of these sums as received from Gosling and Sharp—he has not accounted to me for either of these sums.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Your's is the the Digestive Bread Depot? A. Yes—all my servants have not robbed me—the prisoner was first deficient in his accounts in 1841—I never kept account of deficiencies
with the prisoner and charged him interest—in 1841 the amount of 50 l. odd was found out against him—I told him I must apply to a Magistrate—he said, if I kept him on, he would pay 8 s. a week, and become a new man—his wife was not in my employ then—she had been—he continued making payments of 8 s. and 9 s. a week down to the time he was taken—it amounted to upwards of 50 l.—he had got a very excellent business in a china-shop—he solicited all my customers, and got their orders—he told me himself it was a thriving concern—Hannah Wissencroft lived in my service about twelve months—she had not a little baby with her when she went away—I had no particular quarrel with the prisoner—I was very angry with him when I found out that he was a thief, and it was his own proposition, to save him from disgrace, if I would keep him—since that offence he became a tee-totaler and a most pious man—he was steady and industrious—I had persons of the name of Newton and James in my service—they were deficient, and when I found them out I turned them away—I did not take 20 l. from the prisoner besides the 50 l.—I am quite sure of that, if he made a wrong entry, he rectified it.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Nine Months.
There was another indictment against the prisoner, to which he pleaded GUILTY.

1341. WILLIAM HOLMES , was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of March, 1 coat, value 16 s., the goods of William Stevenson.

Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. I believe there were a great many other persons there? A. Not at the time it was taken—I think only the prisoner and another man.

1342. JOHN CORDWELL, WILLIAM MORRIS, WILLIAM COLLIER , and EDWARD SHIELDS , were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Simpson, on the 13th of March, at the township of New Brentford, and stealing therein 40 knives, value 1 l. 18 s., the goods of George Simpson, and that Morris had been before convicted of felony.
GEORGE SIMPSON . About half-past eight o'clock in the morning of the 13th of March I left Brentford for London. I have a shop at Brentford, where I sell knives—I left my son-in-law to mind it—I came home at eight at night, and
found a square of glass cut—there was a hole about one and a half inch long and three quarters wide, and a tray that was full of knives in the morning, had only three left in it—about three dozen were gone—I have seen about a dozen of them since, and one of them I can swear to—the tray was close to the window—my door was left open all the morning—I will swear to this one knife—I have lost knives like these fourteen, and I believe these to be mine.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Where do you live? A. At Brentford. I sleep at this house—it is in the parish of Hanwell, and in the town-ship of New Brentford—the knives could have been got out very easily by a wire, without putting the hand in—I will not swear that these knives never belonged to Mr. Baines, at Hammersmith—I believe he is here on some trial—I will not swear that these do not belong to any cutler in London.
JOHN HADDON ( police-constable T 46.) I received information, and made inquiries—I got into an omnibus, and overtook the four prisoners about six that evening—I got out and saw Morris throw four knives and a pot of marmalade over a garden—I picked up the four knives—I showed them to Mr. Simpson, and he identified them—I found on Collier seven knives, a pot of marmalade, this piece of wire, and some other things.
WILLIAM MARSH ( police-constable T 62.) I went and took Shields to the station—I found on him four knives, and one knife was given me by a boy, which he said was flung away—I have shown them to Mr. Simpson, and he believes they are his.
RICHARD HANCOCK ( police-sergeant T 10.) I took Cordwell about ten minutes before nine o'clock the same evening. I went to Paddington after him—he said he had never been to Brentford, and did not know where it was—I told him I took him for a robbery at Brentford, and told him what I had been told by Shields—he said he would tell the Magistrate all about it.
ROBERT NASH ( police-constable S 221.) I produce a certificate of Morris's former conviction, which I got from Clerkenwell—( read)—he is the person.
(Collins and Shields received a good character.)
CORDWELL,— NOT GUILTY .
MORRIS,— GUILTY of stealing only. Aged 14.— Transported for Seven Years.—Parkhurst.
COLLIER,— GUILTY . Aged 14.
SHIELDS,— GUILTY . Aged 10.
Of stealing only.— Confined Nine Days, and Whipped.

1843. WILLIAM CLARKE , was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 4 shillings, the monies of Thomas Marriott, his master.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS MARRIOTT . I keep a public-house in High-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner was in my service not quite a month—I was induced to take him by a person—he said he had got no money—he was employed to take money at the bar—on the 28th of March I marked four shillings and put them into the till—I missed them—after that I marked other money, and on the 2nd of April I sent for an officer and had the prisoner taken—his box was searched, and 5 l. worth of silver was found in one bag, and two papers of silver in another box, containing 2 l. and some odd shillings in a purse, 7 l. 10 s. 10 d. in all—among it I found these four shillings which I had marked and put into the till on the Tuesday.
HENRY BERESFORD ( police-inspector.) I was called, and found these 4 s. in the 5 l. of silver.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.

1344. ROBERT BAKER , was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1 pair of boots, value 6 s.; 2lbs. weight of bread, 3 d.; 8 shillings, 2 pence, 4 halfpence, and 1 farthing, the property of James Gifford.
JAMES GIFFORD . I live in Cross-street, Huntingdon-street, Hoxton. On the 4th of April I left my boots safe—the next morning I found ray breeches, trowsers, waistcoat, and boots behind the door on the bannister—my father sleeps with me—the money stated was in my pockets—some one had come into my room and taken these things—the prisoner did live in the house for some time—there is a man in the house who plays instruments—he is out late, so we leave the street-door unfastened, and on that night I had forgotten to bolt the roomdoor—I had seen the prisoner at six and half-past ten o'clock, before I lost the property, and saw him in the Hackney-road the next morning—I told a policeman—he took him, and found eight shillings and 2 1/2 d. on him, and one shilling I could swear to—this is it—he had told me on the Saturday that the worst had come to the worst, as he had gone to the relieving officer—he pulled out a paper to go and break the stones.
Prisoner. Q. Is it not as likely that the shilling might have got chafed against my tobacco-box as yours? A. No, there is a black mark on its edge, and there are three scratches on the other shilling I had in my bag—the money found on him exactly corresponds with what I had, all but 1 1/2 d.
WILLIAM JACKSON ( police-constable N 190.) I took the prisoner, and found on him eight shillings and 2 3/4 d., and some papers and books—the prosecutor said he lost eight shillings and 4 3/4 d.—he tried to keep his money back, and gave me a shilling, but I found a purse on him, containing seven shillings—he had got the purse up the sleeve of his shirt—the prosecutor immediately identified this shilling, as being chafed and marked—the prisoner had a quantity of bread.
Prisoner. I don't know anything about it.
GUILTY . * Aged 29.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18430403-1345]
1345. THOMAS JARVIS , was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 200lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l., the goods of Robert Ford Hayles; and fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.—4 other COUNTS, stating it to be the property of William Hubbard, and William and Sophia Hubbard.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH BUCK ( police-constable F 124.) On Sunday evening, the 19th of March, my attention was attracted to a house, No. 2, Bedfordbury, in the parish of St. Martin-in-the-fields—some bricks had fallen from the cornice-work—on the following Monday I went and examined the state of the lead, it was coiled up when I first saw it, but would require cutting before it could be taken away, and the place where the lead had been was vacant—I saw the prisoner at the window—he was within reach of this lead—I went round my beat, and returned about twenty minutes past six o'clock—I then noticed that the lead was beaten out, and flattened down to its place—I came again at half-past seven o'clock—I saw a truck, and the prisoner brought a barrel out of the house, put it into the truck, and pushed the truck round to King William-street, from the front of the house, and left it there—I looked at it, and found the barrel contained some lead—it was much heavier lead than that which I had noticed before—I then saw the prisoner at the barrel—I asked him what he had there—he said "Nothing, it is my own property"—I was not satisfied, and took him to the station—I went back to the house, went on the roof, and found the dormer had been stripped of lead—the cornice was left as it was—there were four nail-holes in the lead.
and there were four nails on the dormer—they appeared to correspond with the holes—I measured them.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. King William-street is some distance from Bedfordbury? A. About half a quarter of a mile—I was standing at the corner of Chandos-street, and Bedfordbury, when he drew the truck away from the door—he had an opportunity of seeing me—I have not had any conversation with anybody who has got up this prosecution, since I was before the Magistrate—I went to Mr. Humphreys the same evening—my evidence was written down before the Magistrate—I did not mention at Mr. Humphreys', for the first time, that I had been to the house, and examined the lead—it was before the Magistrate—I believe I said at Mr. Humphreys', for the first time, that I saw the prisoner move the truck into the back-street—I followed behind him when he was drawing the truck—he had left the truck for about a moment, when I examined it—I did not see where he went to—he did not tell me he was going to have the lead recast—it was at Mr. Humphreys' I first mentioned about the nail-holes—Mr. Elliott was not there at the time—I did not see him there at all—I went to the house afterward—I was in my police dress—my examination before the Magistrate was read over to me—I stated that it contained all I wished to say.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Mr. Humphreys is the solicitor for the prosecution? A. Yes—I attended there, and had my examination taken down—I was desired by Mr. Humphreys to come to his office.
WILLIAM HUBBARD . I am the husband of a lady whose name was Sophia Hayles—she was a widow, and I married her, fourteen or fifteen years ago—she had a son by her first marriage—he is not of age—I and my wife have received rent for this house in Bedfordbury since our marriage—there was formerly a tenant named Spronton there—when he went out he let the prisoner in—the prisoner has paid me rent—I have had very great difficulty in getting it from him, and have been obliged to seize his goods several times—at last I was obliged to bring an action in my own name—I have not received anything in consequence of that action.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner has been in the house twelve years, I believe? A. About eleven—I hold the title deeds of this house—the title to it has not been in dispute, to my knowledge—I went to Mr. Humphreys, at the last examination—I was before the Magistrate the day the prisoner was committed.
WILLIAM ELLIOTT . I am clerk to Mr. James Taylor, who is agent to this property—this house belongs to the son of Mrs. Hubbard before her marriage—he is heir-at-law—Mr. Hubbard and his wife have been in the receipt of the rents since their marriage—an action was brought for use and occupation in consequence of not getting the rent—I attended at the Master's office to tax the costs—the prisoner appeared in his own behalf—the costs were taxed at 100 l. and some odd shillings—the prisoner said he was not prepared to pay the amount, he should want time—I told him there was a large arrear of rent due, and he had better give up the premises—he said he was willing to do so—I told him Mr. Hubbard, I had no doubt, would make a great sacrifice if he would give up the possession of the property; he might give it up at Lady-day—he said he would.
Cross-examined. Q. How long is it since you first received rent of the prisoner? A. Between ten and eleven years—he was not to put the house in order, or repair it—I was to write to Mr. Hubbard to know what he would take off.
JOHN BUCKLEY . I am a plumber, in Bath-street, City-road. I prepared
the lead-work for the repair of this house in Bedfordbury some years ago—I have examined this lead—I got into the house the first time after the lead was removed—I noticed the dormer window on the roof—I have since seen the lead in possession of the officer, and, in my judgment, it was taken from the place—I have measured the nails and nail-holes, and it exactly corresponds—I went with the lead to the house, and they refused to let us in—there is about a hundredweight and a half of lead gone—this is not more than three-quarters of a hundredweight—this piece is worth 7 s. or 8 s., and the whole perhaps 21 s.—it would cost a great deal more to replace it.
Cross-examined. Q. But if that had been re-cast, with a little more, and replaced, it would cost less? A. It would depend on the substance—I knew nothing of the premises before the repairs in 1836.
COURT. Q. Does this appear to require re-casting? A. No—it would have lasted fifteen or twenty years.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18430403-1346]
1346. MARIA BREDON , was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 bottle, value 2 d.; and 1/2 a pint of brandy, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of George Budd, her master; to which she pleaded

1347. WILLIAM GUNN , was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of March, 135 newspapers, value 2 l. 13 s. the goods of Herbert Ingram and another, his masters.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM DANIEL STEVENS . I am warehouseman to Palmer and Clayton, printers of the "Illustrated London News," Crane-court, Fleet-street. On Saturday, the 18th of March, about eleven o'clock in the day, the prisoner came to me for nine quires—he was then in the service of the owners of the paper—about an hour after he came for five more, or 135 copies—I gave him a delivery ticket for them—in the due course of business they should have been delivered to his employers—he did not bring me a ticket—he was to get one part of it signed by the publisher, and bring that part signed back to me—he did not bring the ticket of the five quires back—he did the nine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Is it the practice for the person who takes the things to bring back the ticket? A. Yes.
WILLIAM LITTLE . I am publisher of the "Illustrated London News," and live in the Strand. The prisoner was in the proprietors' employ—on Saturday, the 18th of March, the prisoner was employed to bring copies from the printer's—at half-past eleven o'clock that morning he brought me a quantity—I do not know how many—he brought a delivery-ticket which I signed, and delivered to him—he did not bring me any more to my recollection, there were so many deliveries—if he brought five quires more I do not remember seeing him more than once that day—he had no authority to sell any copies—135 copies are worth 2 l. 13 s. 9 d.—he did not come back after that Saturday.
SAMUEL WILSON . I am a newsman, in the employ of John William Thomas Newthwaites, of Pope's Head-alley. Between four and five o'clock on Saturday afternoon, the 18th of March, the prisoner came and asked if I wanted any "Illustrated London News"—he said he came from the publisher's—our boy had been at the office, and could not get any copies on account of the sale for them—thinking it strange, we questioned him—he
said he came from Mr. Little's, and if we did not take them he must take them elsewhere—I purchased all he had got, which was forty-seven sheets—I paid him 18 s. 9 d., or 5 d. a paper—I paid the fair trade price for them—from the mode in which he behaved it threw me off my guard.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. Never—I had him in conversation two or three minutes, but I can tell him again by his face.
HERBERT INGRAM . I am one of the proprietors of this newspaper—I have one partner—I engaged the prisoner as errand-boy six or seven months—he met me, and said that Mr. Little said he would not employ him again, he was lazy—I said I had not discharged him, and engaged him again.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this paper registered, like others? A. Yes.
THOMAS SAWYER . I am in the service of my father, a news-agent, of Pope's Head-alley. About four o'clock in the afternoon of the 18th of March, the prisoner came to me to sell some "Illustrated London News"—I bought two quires for 1 l. 1 s. 6 d.—that is a fair trade price.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. Yes, on the Saturday before—these papers are numbered—I do not know the numbers which mine were—I know the dates.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1348]
1348. JAMES SAMS , was indicted for embezzlement.
ELIZABETH SADLER . I am the wife of George Sadler, of Queen's-mews, Pimlico. On the 18th of March the prisoner came with some beef—I paid him 6 s. 5 d.—I do not know who for—I had never seen his master.
Cross-examined by MR. WYLDE. Q. Have you a book in which the items are marked down? A. No—I had a ticket, but no receipt.
JAMES COWELL . I am a butcher. The prisoner was in my service, and it was his duty to pay me the same day as he received money—I sent him with some meat for Mr. Sadler, on the 18th of March, which came to 6 s. 5 d.—he did not bring back the money—it was his duty to do so.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not he in the habit of taking out meat to sell to customers, which he himself only knew? A. No—he ordered meat, and booked it to Mrs. Sadler—I did not know her then—he was not in the habit of taking out meat, selling it, and giving part of the profits to me—my wife received 2 l. odd from him on the 22nd of March—previous to this I had no customers in the Palace till he got them—I knew that he had one there of the name of Bloss—he used to receive the money from them, and account to me for it.
JANE COWELL . I am the wife of James Cowell. I received 2 l. 5 s. from the prisoner, for a bill he had taken in, but not from Mrs. Sadler.
Cross-examined. Q. What is that book in your hand? A. The book in which I book the meat to my customers—here is the account of 2 l. 5 s.—the prisoner was not in the habit of taking out meat and selling it on our account until he got the order—he did not bring us many customers—on the 18th he said that he should leave us—I had had no settlement with him after the 18th—I asked him who was to pay the remainder of the money which he took out meat for—it amounted to 2 l. 13 s. 3 d.—he put it down to Mr. Sadler, and others.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1349]
1349. MARY SWEENY , and ANN M'CARTHY , were indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of March, 3 shirts, value 1 l.; and 1 snuff-box, 2 l.; the goods of Charles Antoine Paupierre.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Antoine Paupierre.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES ANTOINE PAUPIERRE ( through an interpreter.) I live in Smith-place, Wapping. About half-past eleven o'clock at night, on the 3rd of March, I met the prisoners between Hermitage-bridge and Wapping—they asked me to pay for a pint of beer—I said I had no money, but if they would come to my room I would give them some out of a barrel—they both took my arm, and went home with me—on arriving there the table was laid, and the soup was all ready—they supped, and drank some gin out of a tea-pot, as much as they liked—after supper I said I was going to bed, I felt tired, and if they liked to come to bed they might—M'Carthy went to bed—Sweeny sat by the fire—I went to sleep directly, as I was a little heady—I awoke about three in the morning—I looked right and left, and felt right and left, and felt nobody there—I jumped out of bed, and went to look for my snuff-box—I missed it, and three shirts—I gave information to the police—I went to a public-house in the neighbourhood with the police, and saw Sweeny there—I saw Murray find my shirts and snuff-box in a house.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What business are you? A. I am foreman to a company for making flour—I have lived in this neighbourhood six months—it was cold gin in the teapot, not hot whiskey.
WILLIAM NORMOYLE , ( police-constable H 133.) At four o'clock in the morning of this day, I saw the prosecutor with sergeant Brook, who could not understand him—in consequence of what he told me in his own language, I went to his house—he gave a description of two women—I knew them—I found Sweeny at Skipper's public-house about six in the morning—the pro-secutor recognized her directly as one who had robbed him—I told her he accused her of stealing his snuff-box and shirts—she said she knew nothing about it—she said she had been in the room and supped there, and he made them drunk—I took her to No. 6, Blue Anchor-yard to see after M'Carthy—I stood at the door with Sweeny while the other officer and the prosecutor went in—he found the shirts and snuff-box—the prosecutor said, "They are my shirts," and on unfolding one of them, the snuff-box was in it—I went to the watch-house with Sweeny and the property—I went back to look for the other prisoner, between seven and eight o'clock—Murray knocked at the door, and M'Carthy came out of the back part of the house, partly undressed—I took her as she was going into an opposite house—she said she was drunk, and had no recollection of any thing of the kind.
Cross-examined. Q. Who is Mrs. Stewart? A. She rents the house—it is a house of ill-fame—I have heard Stewart has been convicted.
THOMAS MURRAY ( police-constable H 134.) I produce the things. Stewart gave me the key of a box after I took Sweeny—I found these three shirts in a box in her room.
JANE STEWART . I keep this house in Blue Anchor-yard. It is a house where M'Carthy lived for five weeks—Sweeny. I have known for five or six years—she knocked at my door about four this morning—my husband let her in—she said she had had a few words with her husband, and asked to leave a bundle with me, as she was afraid he would tear them—I told her to put them on the table—she said it would be safer to put them in the box—the box was unlocked—she put them in, locked it, and gave me the key—at a quarter past six the policeman came with her—I gave them the key of the box—I said she had been there—M'Carthy was out all that night till she was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known M'Carthy? A. Three or four years—I once had six months for striking a man with a quart-pot in my own defence—it is seven yean ago—I did not represent myself as M'Carthy's sister at the prison—I came here to bring her some clean clothes—I passed myself as her sister.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Could you get more easily at her by saying you were her sister? A. Yes—the turnkey refused me the first day.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
SWEENEY,— GUILTY . Aged 28.
M'CARTHY,— GUILTY . Aged 30.
Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430403-1350]
1350. MARTIN SLATER, WILLIAM WALSH , and EDWARD BUXTON , were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 200lbs. weight of carrots, value 6 s.; and 200lbs. weight of pieces of carrots, 4 s.; the goods of William Davis.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES SMITH . I am clerk at Black Lion Wharf, Lower East Smithfield. About the 9th of March the smack Waterloo, Captain Laws, arrived there partly laden with carrots, consigned to Mr. Davis, of Spitalfield's-market—they were carted away by Davis's carts—there were about four sacks left—there were some other carrots from another ship lying at the wharf, belonging to Mr. Davis—I saw a man named Coburn, and soon after Buxton and Walsh came to the wharf and took some sacks of carrots away—I do not know how many—there were no carrots belonging to anybody but Mr. Davis on the wharf on the 15th of March.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I was the consignee of these carrots—the three prisoners have occasionally worked for me, but not on the 15th of March—they had no authority to go to the wharf and take these four sacks of carrots—there were some refuse in another sack—they are worth 10 s. or 12 s.—Slater came to me for money the day before—he said there were a few rotten carrots in the vessel, which the captain said he might have—I said they were not the captain's to give away, if they were rotten they ought to be thrown, away, but being consigned to me, they ought to be put on shore—I told him at his peril to take them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOWLING. Q. Slater was the only person there? A. No, they were all there—I addressed it to all of them—I think they all heard it.
JAMES WOODWARD . I am a green-grocer, and live in Long-lane. On Wednesday, the 15th of March, I went to the Black Lion Wharf to cart away some carrots of Davis's—I saw the prisoners there between eight and nine—Slater said, "These are the sacks you can have for 6 s."—there were two sacks and a half of carrots, and one sack of refuse—I went away, and said I would not give more than 5 s.—my porter came to me, and said, "Give me the 6 s.," and I bought them—I asked Davis's man to cart them with the carrots I bought of Mr. Davis—he said he would not—Buxton and Walsh were on the wharf—Slater said the captain gave them to them for clearing out the vessel—Buxton and Walsh loaded them into my cart, and Slater sold them to me, and took the money—they weighed rather better than 300 cwt. and a half.
Cross-examined. Q. These were rubbish, were they not? A. Yes.
(Slater and Buxton received a good character.)
SLATER,— GUILTY . Aged 32.
WALSH,— GUILTY . Aged 25.
BUXTON,— GUILTY . Aged 30.
Recommended to mercy.
Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18430403-1351]
1351. MARGARET CONNOR , and JAMES CONNOR , were indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 17th of March, 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 watch-guard, 15 s.; 1 locket, 1 s.; one pair of boots, 6 d.; and 1 cap, 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Spong; knowing them to have been stolen.
ANN SPONG . I am the wife of Thomas Spong, and live in Buckle's Cottages, Upper Clapton. I found Elizabeth Loader, took her into the house, and befriended her—she suddenly went away, on the 17th of March, while I was gone out—I missed a watch and chain, and locket—she would have an opportunity of taking them—I never saw either of the prisoners before—Loader never came back.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How do you know the watch? A. I have had it ten years—here is "Robert Stroud" on the back of it, and one of the hands is turned up at the end—I have the key I wind it up with.
WILLIAM HOWARD . I am a pawnbroker. This watch was pawned on the 18th of March, by Margaret Connor—she first asked 10 s., and afterwards 15 s.—I lent her 15 s.—she said it was her husband's—I asked what he gave for it—she said he bought it before marriage, and she could not say—she was sorry she was obliged at last to part with it, in consequence of the illness of her husband—this guard-chain was pawned by James Connor, on the 20th of March—my brother asked him if it was silver—he said he bought it for silver.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known Margaret? A. Nearly six months—the prisoners are husband and wife—they bear a very good character—James is very ill, and Margaret gets her living by keeping a coffee-stall.
JOSEPH NIFTON ( police-constable D 118.) On Monday, the 20th of March, I went to No. 20, Carlisle-street, where the prisoners lived—I saw Margaret first—I said I came about a watch she had pledged—she said the had pledged it; she found it in the Edgeware-road—she produced this cap, and said she found this watch-chain and locket in the cap on the morning of the 18th, close against her coffee-stall—I asked if she knew a girl named Elizabeth Larey, or Loader—she hesitated a few moments, and at last said, "No"—James then said, "We don't know anybody"—Margaret produced the duplicate of the property—I told her she had done very wrong in pledging them, if she had found them—I took her into custody—James being very ill, I did not take him—after the examination the next morning I went back to the prisoners' lodgings with my brother officer, and found this cap, bonnet, boots, and shawl, which she said Loader wore when she absconded.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it after James said, "We don't know any one," that Margaret said she did not know? A. No, before—she made the reply first.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
(The prisoners received a good character.)
MARGARET CONNOR,— GUILTY . Aged 47.— Confined Three Months.
JAMES CONNOR,— NOT GUILTY .

MARY ANN TURNER . I am servant to Samuel Soane, of Bethnal-green. He gave me 6 s. 6 d. in silver, some copper, and three duplicates, and directed me to get three parcels out of pledge—I gave the duplicates and money to the prisoner on Saturday, the 1st of April—there was a half-crown and four shillings—I told her to be as quick as she could—she ought not to have been above an hour—she did not come back—I did not see her till the Monday
when she was in custody—the prosecutor is the father of the prisoner—he makes me make the money good.

GEORGE HANSON . I am shopman to Thomas Smith, a pawnbroker—I took in these coats of a female—I cannot tell who.

JOHN HARRIS . I am shopman to George Simpson, who lives in Crawford-street. On Saturday, the 1st of April, the prisoner came into the shop, between five and six o'clock—she asked to look at some black sarcenet ribbons—I showed her some—she threw her cloak over a part of the drawer where the ribbons were—she took up a piece of ribbon, dropped it back into the drawer, and asked the price of another ribbon—I told her 2 d. a yard—she threw her cloak again over the drawer, took another piece, and put it under her arm—she said they were too dear, and walked out—I went out after her, brought her back, turned her cloak on one side, and found this ribbon under her arm—it is my master's.

1356. MARTHA GREENFIELD , was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of March, 1 pair of shoes, value 1 s.; the goods of Catherine Nicholls, her mistress.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
CATHERINE NICHOLLS . I am a widow, and live in Canonbury-terrace, Islington—the prisoner came into my service on Wednesday, the 29th of March,—on the Sunday afternoon she was to go to church—I sent my son to say it was too soon to go then, it was only half-past two o'clock—she then came in, and asked to speak to me—she said, "I have got a pain in my side; will you let me go to the chemist's?"—I said, "Martha, you are not ill; and if you quit my house now, you will not return again"—she went, and I did not see her again till Tuesday evening, about eight o'clock, when she came with a man—I let her in, but not the man—she said, "I have come for my box"—I said, "Your manner has been so strange, and your conduct to mysterious, that I beg to open that box before you take it away "—she said, "O yes," and opened it with a key, which she took from her side—she commenced taking the things out—my youngest son held the candle—both my sons were present—she appeared to wish to conceal a small parcel in a worsted handkerchief—she held it in her hand, and took a gown out and threw it over it—she was going to take something else out—I said, "What is that you have underneath the gown"—(I saw there was something shining out)—she said, "Oh, a pair of boots; a pair of shoes that Mrs. French gave me, (who was a mistress she had formerly lived with) and you may come with me to see"—she opened the handkerchief, and I saw these shoes—I knew them to be mine; but they have been defaced in the binding—I had never given them to her, or authorized her to take them—I said, "Oh, you wicked girl; you quite distress my feelings, as a mother, to see you do such a thing"—she threw the shoes on the floor—I took them up, and my son said they were mine—she said, "As a mother, you will forgive me"—I sent my son for a policeman, and while he was gone she said, "They are your's"—they are worth 1 s.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did she not complain that she could not get enough to eat? A. No; my feelings got much excited before the Magistrate, and I insisted on his committing her for trial.
JOHN BROWN ( police-constable N 180.) The prisoner was given into my custody for stealing these shoes—she said she did not mean to keep them.
GEORGE NICHOLLS . I am the prosecutrix's son—I heard the prisoner tell my mother, she had put them in her box, seeing her box open in the room.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430403-1357]
1357. JOSIAH SINGLETON , was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 327lbs. weight of coffee, value 18 l. 8 s.; and 3 bags, 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Green; and CHARLES WILSON , for feloniously inciting, procuring, and commanding John Singleton to do and commit the said felony; and ELIZABETH ABURROW , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to be stolen, against the Statute, &c.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS GREEN . I am a grocer and tallow-chandler, and live in Leather-lane—on the 23rd of March I entrusted Wilson with a quantity of coffee to be roasted—it was to be returned next morning—about two o'clock in the afternoon of the 24th, he came and said his horse, cart, and harness had been stolen, with my coffee in the cart—I asked where—he said, "From the corner of George-street, Minories"—I asked him why he should take my coffee to the Minories, seeing he roasted it in Old-street—he said he had to call on Mr. Josephs—I asked him for what purpose—he said, "To see if they wanted any coffee roasted"—I said, "Why not leave my coffee first?"—he gave me
no satisfactory reason—I asked for the keys of the stable—he said they were in the cart, and they were stolen—I asked if he kept any man—he said, "Yes, one"—he did not know his name—he called him Joe —I said, "Where does he live?"—he said, "I don't know"—I said, "When did you see him—I said, "—he said, "This morning; he cleaned my harness and loaded the cart"—I said, "What with; my coffee?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Which way did you come?"—he said, "At Moorgate; I got a ticket, but I lost it."
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did not he say that he should make it good by instalments of so much a-week? A. He asked if I would accept 10 s. a-week—I told him I bettered he stole it, and would not do anything of the kind—I sent to Josephs, and found it was a true account; but they had not seen his coffee, nor did they give him any coffee—they were opening a sewer, which would prevent a cart going dose to the house.
JAMES COOK . I am the prosecutor's shopman—on the 22nd of March I weighed three hundred-weight of coffee to be roasted—it was in three bags—there was a delivery note with it—I desired Wilson to bring it back with the coffee.
MARY BECK . I live in Harrold's-place, John's-row, St. Luke's, which is next door to Wilson's stable—between nine and ten o'clock on Friday morning, the 24th of March, I saw Wilson go into the stable—shortly after I saw Singleton—I afterwards saw a cart and horse go out with some bags—Singleton was driving—Wilson had gone on a few minutes before.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. What was in these bags you cannot say? A. No. THOMAS WELLER. I live in New-street, Borough-road—I let a cart to Singleton on Thursday, the 9th of March—he returned it on the 24th of March, from a quarter to ten minutes to one—my cart bad my address on it—I did not let him a horse—he took the horse away with him.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When did he pay you? A. He gave me 3 s. when he hired it, and 3 s. more on the 22nd.
JOHN HALES . I am collector of the City tolls at Moorgate—on the 24th of March, about a quarter past eleven o'clock, there was a horse and cart driven through my gate by Wilson—I gave him a ticket that was marked "Moorgate; City tolls, 243"—I did not take any notice of the load.
GEORGE EDINGTON . I am collector of the City tolls in Jewry-street, Minories—about ten minutes to twelve on the 24th of March, a cart and horse passed through my gate—a ticket was produced, with "Moorgate, City toll" on it—the cart appeared to be laden at the top with coffee—I did not see how many bags—I cannot say who was driving.
Cross-examined by MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you see any coffee? A. No, I could not see what it was—I know by the smell it was coffee—I stopped the cart promiscuously and demanded the toll—I did not notice the cart nor horse, or the man—I jumped up behind and felt the bag—it felt like coffee.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you able to say it was coffee? A. I think it was—there was a scent of coffee in the cart at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You have been examined about this before? A. Yes—I know Wilson was not the man who was with the cart.
ELIZABETH FULLERTON . I am landlady of the house No. 14, Pearl-row, Blackfriars-road. I know Singleton by sight—between twelve and one o'clock, on Friday, the 24th of March, I saw him come with a horse and cart to my door, to Aburrow, who has my front room—he carried up something, I do not know what—he came down, and drove off—soon after he came back with the horse, took off the harness, and went up stairs—it would take him five or ten minutes to go to the Borough-road.
JOHN MITCHELL ( City police-constable, No. 519.) On the 24th of March
Wilson was given into my custody—I went to his stable, in Harrold's-buildings—I got over the wall, and searched them—I produce a paper I found on a nail there—it is the weight and quality of the coffee—I went that night to Jermyn-street, St James's—the landlord let me in—I went into the room pointed out as Wilson's—I found two boys there—I went again at three o'clock in the morning, and waited at the door till Singleton came out—I found he was there by speaking to him—I did not take him into custody till the Sunday afternoon—I found some keys—I have tried them to the stable door, and found they fitted—I went into Aburrow's room, in Pearl-row, Blackfriars-road, and found this bag—it did not contain coffee, but I found some coffee there in a bag—Aburrow was at borne—I asked her who brought the coffee there—she said, Singleton, on Friday, and it was no use denying it—the coffee was under the bed—I took Aburrow on the following Friday.
THOMAS GREEN re-examined. I cannot say anything about the ticket produced by the officer—I asked Wilson whose cart and horse it was—he said the cart he had hired of Mr. Thomas Wetton, of the Borough, and the horse was his own.
JAMES COOK re-examined. This is the ticket which was given out, and ought to hare been returned with the coffee—this coffee is similar to that entrusted to Wilson—it has been roasted—this is the bag I gave him the Java coffee in—it is the original bag—I have the warrant to prove it in my pocket.
NOT GUILTY .
