1359. MARK NAPIER , was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of March, 2 sovereigns, the monies of Thomas Finley.—2nd COUNT, stating it to belong to Edward Robinson.—3rd COUNT, to William Rowe.
ELIZABETH FAIRBAIRN . I live in Dalgeish-street, Limehouse. I know Captain Mitchell, of the ship Graham —he sailed last April, and entrusted me to receive 2 l. a month to send to his parents, at Hull—I had a note from Mr. Weldon to the owners of the ship at the counting-house in Cheapside for the money—I received it four or five months, on account of Captain Mitchell—I gave the advance note to Mr. Rowe, the clerk—I have known the prisoner since he was a child—he said he would take the trouble to receive the money for me, as he passed the door of the counting-house every day—he received it for me up to the month of March—he brought it me the first time—I asked him how it was he did not bring it any more—he said he sent it away—I said that was very well—I always asked if he had sent it—he said, "Yes"—I asked him about it in March—he said he bad not got it, as there was a little bother about the ship, but he said, "I will get it to-morrow"—he then did not come for three or four days; in consequence of which, I received a letter from him—I know it to be his writing—it said he was going to remove—I became alarmed about the money, and went with an officer in search of the prisoner, and found him in Norfolk-street—he told the officer he had sent it away by post—at the station he said he had not sent it, but made use of it.
Prisoner. Q. Did I know anything of it before you asked me to fetch it? A. I told you I had it—I did not bring the money to your house the week after you had received it the second time and say I had made use of it—the first time you went to the post-office—I never made use of a penny of it in my life—I called at your house on the 4th of March to see you, and you said you had called for the money—I said, "The next money you draw don't send it away,
I want it,"because I wanted to see it—I said I wanted to take a watch out, and that I was back with my rent, and wanted to make ate of it—I was afraid, because I had seen a change in you.
WILLIAM ROWE . I am clerk to Mr. Robinson, a merchant, in Cheapside. Captain Mitchell is captain of the Graham —he is entitled to draw a monthly note in favour of Mr. Weldon—this note was left with Fairbairn—Mr. Finlay leaves the money for Mr. Weldon, and Fairbairn had to receive it for Weldon, the chief officer of the ship—I paid it to the prisoner in March, for Fairbairn—I should have paid it to her if she had come—she was not well when she came the last time, and I said, "Leave the note with me"—she said, "I will send some one for it"—I said, "Leave me the name," and she left, "Napier"—I put the name in the corner—when the prisoner came he answered the description, and I paid it him—when I paid the money I parted with the reeponsibility—Mr. Finlay had no other responsibility about it, nor any share in it—if anything, it was the property of Fairbairn.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT, Tuesday, April 11 th, 1843.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1361. HENRY GRANT , and JOHN WALKER , were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 2 coats, value 3 l.; 1 gown, 10 s.; 1 shawl, 3 s. 6 d.; 1 handkerchief, 2 s.; 1 ornament, 6 d.; 1 blow-pipe, 3 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of gloves, 6 d.; the goods of Henry Robinson.
HENRY ROBINSON . I keep the Coach and Horses in Cross-street, Hatton-garden. On the evening of the 29th of March, my wife went up stairs to the bed-room to get some change—she called my father out of the club-room, then came down, and called me—I went up, and found Grant under the bed—I fetched the policeman, and when I got him, Grant had been pulled from under the bed—Walker then came in—he called him some name, and said, "You shall not go to the station-house without me; if you take one you shall take both"—these articles were under the bed, and were pulled out with Grant—I had seen them safe under a window in the club-room, which is in the first floor, two hours before—the bed-room is on the second floor—I had seen the prisoners in the club-room before, and I did not observe Walker again till he came into the bed-room, and said, "You shall not go to the station without me."
Walker. Q. Was not I in the club-room when you were called to go up stairs? A. I could not see you then—I saw you afterwards.
MARY ROBINSON . I passed the club-room that evening, but the door was shut—I went to the bed-room, and found Walker under the bed and Grant against the fire-place—I went down stairs, and gave information, and my husband's father went up stairs—I did not go up again—I had left these wings on the table in the bed-room about three hours before I saw the prisoners—this china figure was on the mantel-piece.
Walker. Q. How can you tell it was me under the bed, you said I had a blue coat on? A. No, you had a jacket—it was you—I did not see your face, I saw your dress—you was lying on your face.
COURT ( reading the depositions) "His back was turned towards me, but
I saw his blue coat and dark trowsers; I am sure he is the man." Witness. I believe it was a jacket—it was blue—I am sure he is the man—I am sure the one that was at the fire-place was Grant.
WILLIAM ROBINSON . I went up stairs, and saw Grant under the bed—Walker came in about a minute or two afterwards—I cannot tell from where—I called my son, and a neighbour ran up—he pulled Grant out, and these things, which were all in a bundle under the bed.
HENRY MOORE . I was in the club-room—I heard "Harry" called, and I went up, thinking it was myself—I saw Mr. William Robinson at the door, with a candle—he said, "There are thieves in the room"—I said, "I will find them"—I opened the door, and on looking under the bed I saw Grant—I pulled him out by the legs, and these clothes came out with him—I did not see Walker.
THOMAS DEAN ( police-constable G 37.) I took Grant, and gave him to another officer, while I got the property—Grant took out of his pocket this china image, blow-pipe, and pair of gloves—he did not say anything, but Walker said they should not go one without the other, if one went they should both go—Walker resisted.
Walker. Q. Where did you take me? A. In the room.
JAMES CHURCHYARD ( police-constable G 174.) I saw Grant take these things out of his pocket.
Grant's Defence. Robinson was about to let his house; the new landlord came that evening to look at it; Robinson invited a lot of men to come and drink free of expense—I was invited by a friend; I had occasion to go down stairs; I did not know the way; I mistook the way up, and got into the room, I suppose.
Walker's Defence. I was not in the room; the policeman says he took me in the room, but it was not till I came down, and the landlord said, "Take him, he is an accomplice;" this woman came out of the room, and she must have seen me if I had come down stairs; I did not know this man before that night.
HENRY ROBINSON re-examined. I was about to let the house, but did not invite persons, and did not give them anything.
GRANT,— GUILTY .
WALKER,— GUILTY .
Confined Three Months.

1364. MARY ANN WILLIAMS , was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Feb., 1 cloak, value 5 s.; 1 pinafore, 1 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 1 s.; the goods of Mary Matilda Ebdon, from her person.
SARAH LANE . I live in Bride-lane, Fleet-street. On the 2nd of Feb. the prisoner came to me for a lodging, as I had a bill in my window—in a few minutes the person came in who lives in my first floor, and it was agreed that the prisoner should lodge with her, and pay 10 s. a week for board and lodging—she then asked me to recommend a man to fetch her boxes from the Commercial Club-house at the back of the Bank—I called a man—she agreed to give him 4 s., and he was to fetch them at six o'clock in the evening—he came, and asked if she was ready—she said, "Yes," and she asked me to let my little niece go with her—I said she might, and she did—her name is Mary Matilda Ebdon—she had a cloak, a pinafore, and a handkerchief on, which were her own property—I bought them and gave them to her—her father is dead, and her mother is 160 miles off—I gave her these things for herself—the man came back at eight o'clock and said he could not find them—the child could not find her way back to Bride-lane—she got to her aunt's at Clerkenwell at one o'clock in the morning, and had not these things then.
MARY MATILDA EBDON . I recollect going with the prisoner that day. I am sure she is the person—when she got to the top of the lane she took hold of my arm, and said she wanted to make a call or two before the man came—she took me towards Temple-bar, and to Piccadilly—she took me to five public-houses, and at the last one she forced me to drink—she took me up some court, took off my cloak, pinafore, and handkerchief—she then took me further on to the corner of a street, left me, and said she would return in two or three minutes.
FRANCIS MANBY DICKER . I am a pawnbroker. This pinafore and handkerchief were pawned by the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I never took the child's things off; the policeman has a spite against me.
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430403-1365]
1365. MARY ANN WILLIAMS , was again indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 shawl, value 6 s.; 1 petticoat, 2 s.; 1 handkerchief, 1 s.; and 1 pair of gloves, 6 d.; the goods of Fanny Jane Oborne; and 1 sheet, 6 s.; the goods of Susanna Thorpe; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
FANNY JANE OBORNE . I live with my aunt in Leader-place, Westminster. On the 23rd of March the prisoner came there at a quarter to eight o'clock in the morning—she said she had come from Windsor by the steam carriage, and wanted my aunt to recommend a person to go and fetch her boxes, which we did—the shawl was lying there, and she asked me to let her put it on her shoulders, as she was very cold travelling all night—she then borrowed a shilling and got some rum, and had some luncheon—then she got me to write a note to her aunt, at the Marquis of Lansdowne's—we went out, and she asked me to lend her another shilling, which I did—she went away with the man who was to fetch the boxes, and when I got home I missed my flannel petticoat, handkerchief, and gloves, which had been on the mangle where the shawl was.
ELIZABETH HARMAN . I am a searcher at the station—I produce this flannel petticoat which I found on the prisoner.
FANNY JANE OBORNE . This is my petticoat—there is no mark on it, but I have another like it, and it is my own making.
JOHN BARKER ( City police-constable, No. 466.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—(read)—the prisoner is the person who was then convicted.
GUILTY . * Aged 23.— Transported for Ten Years.

WILLIAM WEBSTER . I reside at Dutchy-wharf—I never gave the prisoner leave to take any iron away, nor lent him any sack to take it with.
Prisoner. Q. Do you deny lending me the sack?—you went into your master's shed, and brought the sack out, and gave it into my hands. Witness. No, I did not.

Prisoner's Defence. I have been fifteen years employed there, and I very often pick up iron out of the mud and on the wharf. I put it on one side; Webster lent me the sack to take it in.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Three Months.

A little after twelve o'clock, on the 7th of April, a young man gave me information, and I missed three pieces of bacon—I ran out, and saw the prisoners turning the corner—I sent my boy after them—the policeman, in the mean time, brought the prisoners and two pieces of the bacon, which were safe ten minutes before.
JAMES DORMAN ( police-constable N 205.) I saw the prisoners running, and each of them had something under his apron—I caught them, and saw Stevens throw down a piece of bacon, and Wood dropped another piece—I took them back to the shop, and one of them dropped another piece—I found a knife on Wood.

[trialAccount t18430403-1371]
1371. SAMUEL CROUCH , was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, 1 set of harness, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of Charles Maccabe; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN KNIGHT . I am groom to Charles Maccabe; his stables are at No. 7, Welbeck-street. On the 27th of March I was at the stable—I left at ten minutes to five o'clock, and there was a chaise harness and a box-coat of my master's there then—I returned at nine, and they were gone—this is my master's harness, which was lost that night—I went to the station, and told Harrison what had happened.
ROGER PARTRIDGE . I am a saddler, carrying on business in Thayer-street. The prisoner lives in Marylebone-lane, near to me—he professes to be a hair-dresser and general dealer—he deals in harness, and clothes, and other things—I heard from the police about this robbery on the Monday, and on the Thursday afterwards I met the prisoner in Marylebone-lane—he said he had got some harness, or knew of some—he said something about 25 s. as the price—I said, "When I see it I shall know whether I will give it"—on the Friday Woodford brought this harness in a hamper, covered over, and soon afterwards the prisoner came—I frequently buy harness in a hamper, and sometimes pack it in hampers—the prisoner came in, and I passed my judgment on this harness—he asked 25 s. for it—I thought it was not worth more than 15 s.—I did not tell him what I would give him for it, as I had
scarcely finished my breakfast—I had often dealt with him before, but a policeman had been with me before, to ask if there had been any harness offered.
Cross-examined by MR. WYLDE. Q. This man lives close to you? A. Yes—I have known him ten years—I have bought many things of him, and he of me—he was a respectable man—I never heard anything against him—I never knew anything wrong of him—I know, at that time, there was a phaeton at his door—I knew of his being a barber.
MR. DOANE. Q. Do you swear he was a respectable man? A. I never knew anything but respectability of him—I have heard that he has been transported—I have heard that in Sept., 1840, he was charged with stealing cloth—I did not know that in August last he was taken up for stealing a watch.
WILLIAM WOODFORD . I am a labouring man, and live in Cross Keys-mews. On Friday, the 31st of March, the prisoner asked me to take a basket of harness to Mr. Partridge's shop—I took it, and left it there—Mr. Crouch came into the shop before I came out—I did not see what was in the hamper—he told me it was harness.
THOMAS HARRISON ( police-sergeant D 14.) I had an intimation of this from Knight, but it was another officer who told Mr. Partridge—I took the prisoner into custody on Friday morning, the 31st—I said, "Crouch, I want you about some harness, the harness you sent to Mr. Partridge this morning"—he said, "I did send some there"—I said, "How did you come by it?"—he said, "I bought it at the door three or four days ago"—he said he gave 25 s. for it—I asked him if he knew who he bought it of—he said no, he did not—I said, "Was any one present when you bought it?"—he said, "No, there was not"—I said it was stolen, and I took him before Mr. Long, the Justice—I gave the same evidence I have now—I was present at the second examination, and the prisoner then called a witness, named M'Laney, who was sworn—he was then examined at the instance of the prisoner—he did not appear at the third examination.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give testimony at the first examination! A. Yes, and what I said was taken down in writing—the Magistrate did not think there was sufficient evidence, and the prisoner was remanded for me to bring forward the man who carried the harness—on the second occasion I made some addition to my deposition—( read)—"On the second examination, Thomas Harrison, being sworn, says, 'When I took him I asked him if any one was present when he bought it; he said, "No, no one was present"—I forgot to mention that yesterday.' "— THOMAS HARRISON.
Q. That was the addition you made next day? A. Yes—the first hearing was on Friday, the 31st; then we all separated, and on the 1st of April I went and made that addition to my statement.
MR. WILDE called
EDWIN MASON . I know the prisoner—I remember seeing him on the 27th of March, at a person's named Crawford, whose place joins my shop—I met the prisoner there that day, about half-past four o'clock, as near as I can recollect—Mr. Crawford is a boot-maker—a person came into Mr. Crawford's who has very often called in—his name is Falday—he asked Mr. Crawford if he was going to stand anything to drink—he said he had no objection, and he sent for sixpenny worth of gin—I should say we remained in Mr. Crawford's shop, as near as I can guess, for three quarters of an hour—the prisoner remained in the house till near eight o'clock.
MR. DOANE. Q. You went to Mr. Crawford's about half-past four o'clock? A. Yes—his shop joins mine, and I continued there three quarters of an hour
—the prisoner was on the premises till eight—Mr. Crawford's shop and mine join together—it is one house—the prisoner and I, and Mr. Falday, were in Crawford's shop about three quarters of an hour—Mr. Falday was then in my room, which is over Mr. Crawford's shop and my shop, till near eight—I am not an assistant to the prisoner, I am his son-in-law—I am a hair-dresser, and so is he—we are both in the same line of business—he lives two or three hundred yards from me—my shop is at No. 8, Bowling-street—the prisoner was in Mr. Crawford's shop first, and I went in when I heard his voice—Mr. Falday then came in, and then there was 6 d. worth of gin sent for—I am sure I cannot say what we talked about—I have not the slightest notion of what I said, or what any one said—I did not sit, I stood—I have known Falday five or six years—I do not know what he is, I only know him from his visiting the shops where I have been—I am satisfied he is not in my line of business—I have always understood him to be a gentleman's servant—he was not dressed in livery, he was dressed as I might be myself—he was dressed in a black coat, and, I think, a black waistcoat—I cannot tell a single thing that was said on that occasion—I cannot tell where I stood in the shop—I am satisfied two or three of them stood—it was in the front shop we had the gin—there was no customer came in while I was there—after we had got rid of the gin, we all went into my room, which is over Mr. Crawford's shop and mine, on the first floor, and we sat down, and played at all-fours—it is my sitting-room and bed-room—we were there, playing at all-fours, from that time till near eight o'clock, and then Falday and Crouch left, and went away—I heard of my father-in-law being charged with this robbery—I did not go and tell the Magistrate about our playing at all-fours—I was not asked any questions—I never told the Magistrate what I could prove—I was there throughout—my father-in-law had a solicitor to attend for him—he never called me—I have lived two years in this shop as a hair-dresser—I do not carry on business in the general line—I never got into trouble myself.
COURT. Q. Who fetched the gin? A. I did myself, in a small black bottle—we drank it out of one glass, what is called a half-quartern glass—we drank it pure, no water nor sugar with it—I had one glass—it was poured out in four different parts—I drank what I suppose would be reckoned a glass, but it was only 1 1/2 d. worth each—there was 6 d. worth between four persons—we did not put down 1 1/2 d. each—Mr. Crawford paid for the gin himself, and it was divided into four parts—the glass was filled four times—I could not say who drank first—there were no toasts drank, but merely, "Here's your health"—I said, "Here is your health" to all round, and I think they drank my health all round—we were not three quarters of an hour drinking this gin—not two seconds hardly—we drank it within two or three seconds—that was at the beginning of the three quarters of an hour, almost as soon as we came into Mr. Crawford's shop—I believe he never has but one chair in his shop at a time, unless he sends for one—it was about half-past four o'clock when we went in—it was twenty minutes or a quarter to eight when Mr. Falday and Mr. Crouch left—I borrowed 4 s. of Mr. Crouch before they left, which makes me remember the time, as I must pay it again—he lent it me in four shillings—I am not aware that anybody saw him give it me—he gave it me in going down the stairs—Falday and Crouch went together—I did not talk to Crouch publicly about this money—I whispered it to him—we had some beer up stairs, which we played for—we played 2 d. a corner each—I do not know who won—I am confident I lost 6 d.—we played three or four games—it did not take three hours to play them—we talked, but I cannot recollect anything that we were talking about—we did not talk about any harness—nothing of that sort—Falday did not tell me what he had lately been doing—Mr. Crawford
was my partner all the evening, and we played for drink—for porter and gin—I cannot state how many quarts of porter we had—it might have been two or three—I do not know how much gin—I suppose not more than one drop, 4 d. worth, or 6 d. worth—my wife fetched it—she was not present all the time—there was no other person present—my wife's name is Mary—they did not all know her name, they called her Mason—they saw her bring the gin in—I had a fire in my room—I did not notice who sat next the fire—there must have been two sitting near the fire—I did not sit near it—my wife drank no tea that evening—she had some gin out of the same glass—I do not believe she had any porter—I do not know—we drank the porter out of the quart pewter pot—we had nothing to eat up stairs—I cannot recollect anything we talked about.
MR. WILDE. Q. Good players don't talk much at cards, do they? A. No.
JOHN CRAWFORD . I am a boot-maker, and live at No. 8, Bowling-street, Marylebone. I remember perfectly well, on the 27th of March, seeing the prisoner—it was from four till half-past four o'clock in the afternoon—he came to my house for a pair of shoes I had been repairing—he had not been there many minutes before Mr. Falday came in, and the prisoner's son-in-law came in, and we had a drop of gin, and then we went up stairs and had a game at cards—we went up about five—I should think Crouch left about half-past seven—I did not pay particular attention to the time.
MR. DOANE. Q. You remember this 27th, very well? A. Yes—it was not impressed on my memory from my friend being charged with a felony—that did not make an impression on me at the time—I know it was the 27th, because my boy's birth-day was the day before—that did not make more impression on me than my friend being charged—I think I heard of his being charged, last Friday was a week—I do not think they sent to me till then—I have known the prisoner three or four years, and they never let me know of it till last Friday week—I did not think much about it—when I was playing at cards, I had not heard of the felony—my attention was about the cards—we all had some gin.
Q. Your customers coming in now and then? A. Yes, pretty fair business—we had the gin in my shop where the customers come—when the customers came in we served them publicly in the shop while the gin was going on—the prisoner and Mr. Falday were customers of mine—no one else came in—I have known Falday, I think, about twelve months—he is a gentleman's servant—I do not know whose service he is in—he lived with some gentleman in Portland-place—he does not bring me jobs from Portland-place—he has brought me work—I do not inquire particularly where a person lives—I never take things home to any residence—I had seen Falday three or four days before, he came in as a friend—a great many people look in on me—the prisoner came in first on the 27th—I have known him about three years—he came for a pair of shoes that I had been repairing—Falday came in first after him, and then the prisoner's son-in-law came in from his place—Falday came for a pair of boots that I had been half-heeling for him—the prisoner's son-in-law came in for nothing—he naturally followed his father-in-law—our two doors join one another—I was not particularly thirsty.
Q. Who proposed the gin? A. Many people have a little drop of gin—I think Mr. Falday suggested it—he said, "Let us have a drop"—those were about his words—we all agreed to it—Mason fetched it—as far as I can recollect, I think I paid for it—I paid 6 d.—we were in the shop about half an hour—I am sure I cannot tell what we were talking about—I cannot
swear we talked about politics—I cannot pretend to tell what we talked about I am sure—I do not recollect whether Falday had any news to tell us from Portland-place—we talked sometimes about one thing and sometimes another—he asked me about making a pair of boots, and what I would charge—I was sitting on my seat which I work on—I work in the shop—I believe all the others stood—mine is a small shop—after the gin, a hand at cards was proposed—I think Mr. Falday proposed it—I will not be certain who did—we went up stairs to play at cards, into Mr. Mason's room, which is directly over my shop—no one else was there I am certain—we played at "all-fours"—we continued till half-past seven—no one came in during the whole of that time, as far as I recollect, neither man, woman, nor child—Mason had no children there, because his wife took them out—there was no female there—we played for 2 d. a corner—I had a partner—we had different partners—Falday was my partner first—we changed several times—we might play a dozen games—I would not pretend to say how many—Mason generally fetched the gin—I did not get particularly drunk—he fetched gin more than three or four times—I cannot say how many times.
GEORGE ROBERTS . I am journeyman to the prisoner, and have been so since before last Christmas—I saw some one bring harness to him on the 28th of March, about nine o'clock—I did not hear him make any offer of the harness for sale—I did not hear what was offered—I saw money pass—I did not hear the person who brought it say any thing to the prisoner—I only saw the harness brought and some money paid.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was this in the morning or evening? A. Morning—my master was not taken into custody at the house—I remember his being in custody—I did not hear what for until Harrison came to our house on the Friday morning—the Friday after the Tuesday I heard he was in custody, not for stealing a harness, but for receiving one—Harrison told me that was the charge—I went to the police-court—when Harrison told me about it, I did not tell him a man brought it there—he did not ask me, and I did not tell him—I did not tell until I was called upon—I knew my master was locked up—I went to the Justice the second time, on the Saturday—I did not give evidence—my master's attorney called me as a witness—I was attending, but was not examined—I heard a witness called by my master of the name of M'Laney—he is not here to my knowledge.
Q. Was anybody present when the man brought the harness? A. There was a gentleman present—I was cutting his hair at the time—he was the only person present—I should know him if I saw him.
Q. That gentleman was not the Mr. M'Laney? A. It must be, sir—I was sent for on the third examination before the Magistrate—I was not called to give any evidence at all.
COURT. Q. The policeman came and told you your master was charged with receiving? A. No, he did not.
Q. You told me you knew he was in prison? A. No, I did not—Harrison came to me on the Friday morning—he had not been there before the Friday to my knowledge—he was not there before my master was taken up to my knowledge.
MR. WILDE. Q. You were cutting M'Laney's hair? A. I was cutting a gentleman's hair at the time.
GEORGE WHITE ( City police-constable 24.) I am the officer attending in the gallery of this Court—I saw this man come out of the gallery and go and speak to one of the witnesses who is outside this Court now—I heard him say, "You must say you had so many glasses, and what it was drank out
of"—this is the man—( pointing to a person named Charlick)—he said, "You must say what you had it out of, and so many glasses, and that will do."
COURT. Q. Bring in the man that he spoke to. A. This is the man, Falday.
JOHN FALKUS . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was not a witness against the prisoner on that occasion, but I was present at his trial—he is the same person who was then tried and convicted, and sentenced to death—I was the prosecutor in another charge against him, and he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 52.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
(The witnesses Partridge and Charlick were committed by the Court.)
Before Mr. Recorder.
KENT CASES.,
[trialAccount t18430403-1372]
1372. JAMES WOODER , was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering a building within the curtilage of the dwelling-house of Walter Allen Meriton, on the 20th of March, at Lee, and stealing therein 1 pair of carriage-lamps, value 1 l.; 1 box, 6 s.; and 1 whip, 4 s.; his property.
GEORGE SWAIN . I am groom to Mr. Walter Allen Meriton, who lives in the parish of Lee. On the 20th of March, early in the morning, I went into the garden adjoining master's stable, and saw the prisoner standing close by the wall, which separates our garden from another—I saw a pair of carriage-lamps and the driving-box at his feet, and a pair of my boots on the top of the wall—I went towards him—he drew a knife, and made an attempt to come at me—I went back, and called "Master"—the prisoner got over the wall into an adjoining garden, and made his escape across the road—I looked stedfastly at him, and am sure he is the man—I went to the coach-house, and found master's carriage-lamps and driving-box gone, which had been safe the night before—the stable is in the yard between the two houses—there is a wall right round, enclosing the garden, coach-house, and dwelling-house—I had locked the front door of the coach-house over night—it was about five minutes before six o'clock when I found the prisoner in the garden.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Was it not much earlier? A. No—I am quite sure it was before six o'clock—I did not look at the clock, but I heard the clock strike directly after—I had never seen the prisoner before—he was dressed in a cloth cap, with a leather front to it, and a white frock on—that is a common dress for a bricklayer.
WALTER ALLEN MERITON . I was formerly a captain in the merchant service, and live at Lee. On Monday, the 20th of March, I saw a person running away from my premises, across the high road—he appeared to have a white short frock on, very clean—I saw the prisoner before the Magistrate next day—he appeared to be dressed the same, but the frock was much darker than when I saw him about 100 feet from my house.
Cross-examined. Q. You never saw him before? A. No—I could not swear to him—I only saw him behind.
CHARLES NINTMO . I am a policeman. I examined the prosecutor's premises, on the 20th of March, at half-past eight o'clock in the morning—I traced some steps in that garden and the adjoining one, and found part of a whip in the adjoining garden.
JOHN CARPENTER . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner about a quarter to nine o'clock, on the Monday evening, at the Hare and Billett,
Blackheath—he said he would accompany me anywhere I pleased, as he had done nothing he was ashamed of—I asked where he was at six that morning—he said he was going to his work, that he left his house at ten minutes before six, and got to his work about five minutes after six, at Mr. Russell's buildings, in Lee-road—that is half or three quarters of a mile from the prosecutor's—I took him to the prosecutor's, and as soon as Swain saw him he said, "He is the man I saw in my master's garden"—the prisoner made no reply.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know he worked at Mr. Russell's? A. I never knew what he worked at—I know his brother to be a bricklayer's labourer—I never knew anything against him—I told the prisoner he was suspected of having committed a robbery—he said, "Me?"—I said, "Yes, and if you know yourself innocent you will not mind accompanying me to near the Lion and Lamb, Lewisham"—he said he would accompany me anywhere, and went—a frock nearly down to the knee is a very common dress—they are slop-frocks—I should know the prisoner from the groom if I saw them apart.
Witnesses for the Defence.
WILLIAM WOODER . I am the prisoner's brother—we live together, at No. 5, King-street, Blackheath. On Monday morning, the 20th of March, he got up at twenty minutes to six o'clock—I called him—he sleeps in the back-room, the next room to me—I told him to make haste, and dress himself, which he did in three or four minutes—we both left the house together, and went to the same place to work—we got to work a little after six—we continued at work together all day, till night—Mr. Meriton's house is about a mile or more from ours, and about a mile from where we work—the prisoner had only been employed at the work on the Saturday and Monday—he ought to be at his work at six o'clock.
COURT. Q. Who saw you at work there that day? A. All the men in the building—we have two clocks in our house, one up stairs and the other down—it would be, I should say, a quarter of a mile out of our way to go to Mr. Meriton's, instead of going direct to our work—I know the time we started—I looked at the church clock on Blackheath-hill—it was exactly ten minutes to six then—it is close to our house, on the right coming down the hill, one side of the clock is towards the road, and one side towards Blackheath—Mr. Bath is foreman of the bricklayers there, but we were at work for the plasterers—Mr. Emly is foreman of the plasterers—he is here—my brother had no knife—he borrowed a chisel of the carpenter to cut his meat with, both on the Saturday and Monday—Mr. Emly and others saw him using it.
JAMES JACKMAN . I am a bricklayer's labourer, and live in Essex-place, Blackheath-hill—I am a neighbour of the prisoner's. On Monday, the 20th of March, I was going across Blackheath, and saw the prisoner and his brother going across the heath, about two minutes before six o'clock, near the Hare and Billett—I knew them both very well for three years and a half or four years—they are hard working industrious boys—the prisoner has been out of work a great deal lately, and I have also—they were going towards Lee—six o'clock was our time to be at work.
COURT. Q. Where were you going to work? A. At Mr. Couchman's, the upholster, in the village—I was wheeling a barrow—when they passed me they were walking in the ordinary manner—they appeared going to their work—I generally saw them going to their work—we often go together—I kept a particular account of this day, because I was told I should have to be here—I went before the Magistrate, but was not called.
THOMAS BULL . I am out of business; I live at the Horse and Groom, on Blackheath-hill. I have known the prisoner since August last—he is a bricklayer's labourer, and has worked for me in that capacity. On the morning of the 20th of March I was standing at my front door, at ten minutes to six o'clock, and saw the prisoner and his brother go quickly out of the yard—I have not the shadow of a doubt about the time—I had looked at the clock—the brother had a brown coat, and a bundle under his left arm—the prisoner had a sort of dirty-white frock and cap on, which is a very common dress—they were going swiftly to their work, in the direction of Blackheath-hill.
COURT. Q. What kind of cap had he? A. A dirty-brown kind of cloth cap—there might be a piece of leather in front—I did not pull out my watch when they passed, but saw the church clock—they came out of the Horse and Groom yard—there is a direct road through there to the place where they lived.
WILLIAM HINDES . I am a master plasterer, and live at Lewisham. The prisoner's brother is employed constantly for me, and I employed the prisoner at intervals—I employed him on Monday, the 20th of March on the Lee-road, at Mr. Russell's buildings—there is a new detached cottage there—it is from three-quarters of a mile to a mile from Mr. Meriton's—you come to the Lee-road first from Blackheath—they would not go near Mr. Meriton's in coming to their work, it would be quite out of their way—they would be nearer Mr. Meriton's when at my house, than they would be afterwards—I expected them both at work that morning—they overtook me, as I was going, at the upper end of Lee-lane—they came close behind me, walking very fast—I heard them talking together—I did not speak to them—that was about two minutes after six—I am very positive to the time, as I heard the clock strike six—they live nearly a mile and a half from the work.
COURT. Q. What clock are you speaking of as striking six? A. Lee church clock—the prisoner had on a sort of dirty-white smock-frock, and a fur cap very much worn, to the best of my recollection—I dined on the premises—I noticed that the prisoner ate his breakfast, on the 18th of March, with a carpenter's chisel, and on the 20th he ate his dinner and breakfast with a carpenter's chisel—there are but few of us in the building—the master I work for ate his dinner with a lath—I had a knife—some of the other men ate with knives—the prisoner's brother ate with a knife with one blade and a half; one blade was broken.
FRANCES WOODER . I am a widow, and live at Blackheath; the prisoner is my son. On Monday, the 20th of March, he was in bed at twenty minutes before six o'clock—I have a clock down stairs, and my son has one up stairs—my clock goes very nearly with the church clock on the hill—the prisoner passed through the room I sleep in, opened the door, and shut it after him—I was in bed—I looked at the clock before he got up, and it wanted twenty minutes to six—I look to see that he goes regularly to his work—I almost always look at the clock—I am quite sure I did so that morning.
GEORGE SWAIN re-examined. It was Lee church clock that I heard strike—it is not half a mile from our house—when the prisoner was brought by the officer to Mr. Meriton's premises I was there, and said I considered he was the man I saw that morning—I said so loud enough for the prisoner to hear me—I did not hear him say a word—I spoke loud enough for all in the house to hear me—the man I saw that morning had on a kind of brown cloth cap.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430403-1373]
1373. JOHN HAWKESWORTH , was indicted for a robbery, with violence, on James Magee, on the 27th of March, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 cap, value 4 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of gloves, 8 d.; his goods.
JAMES MAGEE . I am in the band of the Royal Artillery. On the 29th of March, about ten minutes past eight o'clock, I was on Woolwich Common, in company with a female, on the public footpath, going from Woolwich towards the Cadets'-barracks—I saw two men pass me on the right—after they had passed a considerable distance, they turned back, and opened out, and I felt a rope round me very soon—I extracted myself from it, and pulled myself towards the one that held it—I had hold of it—then the other one, in company with two men, came on me behind—I did not see them previously—they pulled me down, and ill-used me in a shameful manner, they kicked me, and said, " Gag him, give it him"—they allowed me to get up, but, I think, through fear; for when I got on my feet, I observed a gentleman at a short distance, and they ran away—when I got up, I missed a black glazed uniform cap, and a pair of gloves which were inside—I did not know any of the persons then—I can identify the prisoner—I am quite sure he was one—it was rather dark—I had seen the prisoner several times before, but never knew who he was, or what his name was—he must have been one of the two who passed me—I think he was the one that had the rope—I am quite sure he was one of the four.
THOMAS HUNTER . I live in Jackson's-lane, Woolwich Common. I was not on the common on Wednesday last—about half-past eight o'clock, as I was coming up from the barracks towards the White-gate, I met the prisoner, with a cap on his head—it was like a soldier's cap—the top was torn off—it was glazed round the peak—I had known the prisoner by sight before—I am quite sure he is the person I saw with the cap.
LOUIS FERDINAND KELLY . I live at Woolwich—I have been in the Artillery, and was discharged through a hurt. The prisoner lives next door to me—last Thursday, between eleven and twelve o'clock, I saw him in the barrack yard fronting his father's house, with a lightish-coloured cap on his head—I looked at it—it appeared to me to be the cap now produced—it was similar to it—when he saw me he took the cap off, and put it under his arm.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constable R 112.) I apprehended the prisoner on Thursday afternoon, between two and three o'clock, at his father's house—his brother and sister were also in the house—he was concealed under a bed—the windows were all barricadoed up, the shutters and door fastened—I got in by taking two or three tiles off the roof—I told him I apprehended him on a charge of assaulting and stealing a cap from Mr. Magee, one of the bandsmen—he said there were others concerned as well as himself—I took him to the station—next day I searched a privy in front of his father's house, ten or fifteen yards from it, in the yard, where the prisoner was at work in the morning, and there found this cap.
JAMES MAGEE re-examined. I can swear this is my cap—my name is in two places in the inside of it—the whole of it was covered with glaze, similar to this front, but the top is torn off.
Prisoner's Defence. I had nothing to do with the rope—I never had the cap in my possession.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430403-1374]
1374. HENRY JAMES HAMMOCK , was indicted for unlawfully obtaining 2 loaves of bread, some butter and cheese, by false pretences.
JOSEPH RICHARDSON . I am a grocer, and live in the New-road, Woolwich. On the 18th of March the prisoner came and asked for a two-pound loaf, some
cheese, and half-a-pound of butter, which I served him with—I did not know him before—he said they were for Mr. Edgar, who had dealt with me sometimes—on the 21st he came again, and asked for a two-pound loaf, half-a-pound of butter, and half-a-pound of cheese—he said he was to have Dutch cheese, because it was the best he had had the day before—he said they were for Mr. Edgar, and took them away.
Prisoner. Q. Have I not been at your shop half a dozen times? A. I have not seen you—you did not say, "Let me have a loaf, butter, and cheese, and I will get Mr. Edgar to pay for them on Wednesday"—I asked how Mr. Edgar was, and you said, very well, but he was not at home—I should not have given him the things had he not said they were for Mr. Edgar.
WILLIAM EDGAR . I am a linen-draper, and live at Woolwich. The prisoner was in my employ eleven months, and was discharged about two months ago—he was not in my service on the 18th of March—I had frequently sent him to Mr. Richardson's, but he never went—I did not send him there on the 18th or 21st of March—I had forbid him my house before that.
JAMES PARRY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner on the 29th of March.
Prisoner. I was out of place, and in distress; I thought I should be able to get Mr. Edgar to pay for it.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.

1375. JOHN DAVIS , was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of February 1 coat, value 12 s.; the goods of Thomas Pacey Birts.
HENRY BERRY . I am a baker, and live at Woolwich. On the 16th of February I was in the street at Woolwich—I saw the prisoner takes coat from inside the door of Mr. Birt's shop—he walked away with it—I ran into the shop—I afterwards saw him again in High-street, and pointed him out to the policeman, who took him—he is the person.
THOMAS TAYLOR . I am in the service of Mr. Thomas acey Birts, a pawnbroker and salesman in Woolwich. On the morning of the 16th, I hung a coat inside the door—I missed it—it is here—( produced)—I know this is it.
EDWARD PALMER . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—he said he had not been there, he had been at the Golden Cross for two hours, in company with the waiter.
FREDERICK LEIGHTON . I am assistant to Mr. Davis, a pawnbroker—this coat was pledged by a woman on the morning of the 17th.
Prisoner. He said, when the coat was brought forward, that he could not swear to it, he had many more of the same pattern—I was not near the place where the coat was stolen—I had been up at the Golden Cross and was walking down from the market-place.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Strongly recommended to mercy.— Confined Two Months.

1377. JAMES MOORE , was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, I dead fowl, value 2 s., the goods of James Nicholson.
GEORGE WILLIAM WHITE . I am shopman to James Nicholson, he is a poulterer at Woolwich. The prisoner and another party came in while I
was shutting up the shop about two o'clock on Sunday morning, the 19th of March—I served the other person with steaks and sausages—I did not like the prisoner's movements, and when the other had given me a shilling in payment, I went round the counter, and missed a fowl—I saw the head of it under the prisoner's jacket, and after I had given change I went after them—I brought the prisoner back, and charged him with it—he said he did not know anything about it—I called the policeman, went with him, and found the fowl at Mr. Moss's door, which the prisoner had passed just before I took him.
Prisoner. Q. Was I not at the door of your shop when you accused me? A. No, I brought you back from where you had got to—you had a piece of timber in your hand, not under your arm.
JURY. Q. Are you quite satisfied that you saw him with the fowl under his arm going out? A. Quite—the door is narrow, and you are obliged to turn rather sideways to get out—it was not wood that he had under his arm, the wood was in his hand.
JOSEPH HOLLOWAY ( police-constable R 137.) I was called, and found the prisoner in Mr. Nicholson's shop—I afterwards went and found the fowl at the step of the next house—the witness identified it—the prisoner first said he knew nothing about it, but when Mr. Nicholson was called, he said he did it for a lark, and when he was taken to the station-house, he said it was his own.
Prisoner. Q. Do you say that I said I took it for a lark? A. Yes, you had a stick of wood in your hand—I did not say I would help you up —I said you had better be careful not to say anything to criminate yourself.
Prisoner's Defence. The man and I went into the shop; the witness was serving him; I came to the door to go out; I did not go two steps from the door, and was returning again, when he came and accused me of taking a fowl; I said I knew nothing about it; I was not going to run away; if I had taken it it is not likely I would have returned.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1382. ROBERT SMITH , was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of March, 1 shilling, and two sixpences, the monies of Elizabeth Skinner and another; and that he had been twice before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH SKINNER . I am single, and am in partnership with my brother. We keep a baker's shop on Blackheath. On the 6th of March, I had occasion to leave the shop and go into the kitchen—I returned and found the prisoner with his hand in the till—when he saw me he instantly left the shop—I cried, "Stop thief"—I found the till half open—I am quite sure the prisoner is the person—I had examined the till about three minutes before, there was then one shilling, two sixpences, and some copper in it, and when he left all the silver was gone.
Prisoner. She told me to run after the chap. Witness. No; he is the one who took the money from the till—there was another taller than him outside.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) I was on duty—I saw the prisoner running from the prosecutrix' shop—I ran and overtook him at the railway—I said, "Hallo! where are you going?"—he said, "I am looking after a lad who has robbed a till."
JURY. Q. How far was he from the prosecutrix' shop when you first saw him running? A. About 200 yards.
Prisoner. She told me to run after the chap, and I did, and the officer took me—this woman is not the chief witness—the fishmonger that saw the prisoner run away is not here. Witness. He never was brought in question at all—it never was mentioned about him.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—the prisoner is the person—he had three months' imprisonment, and was whipped.
Prisoner. It is false, I never was in prison but once before. Witness. I am sure he is the person—he was tried with another person, and each of them reversed their names—the prisoner gave his name as Robert Hall.
EDWARD PRIME ( police-constable B 85.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person—he was tried in the Old Court—I am sure he is the person.
Prisoner. That is right.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Fourteen Years.

HENRY BRADLEY ( police-constable R 250.) I met the prisoner at past eleven o'clock, on the 17th of March—he had this pair of shoes, which he said he found in the road.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( police-constable R 202.) I saw the prisoner coming out of the tap-room of the Lord Duncan, wrapping something in a handkerchief, and putting it under his frock—there was no one else there—I went into the room, and saw the prosecutor asleep, without his shoes—when the prisoner was brought in with the shoes, I knew him to be the man I had seen.

1384. WILLIAM SHRIMPTON , was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of March, 1 coat, value 7 s., and 1 waistcoat, 1 s.; the goods of Richard Warwickshire.
RICHARD WARWICKSHIRE . I am a green-grocer, and live in Harding's-lane,
Woolwich. On the 20th of March, about nine o'clock, I missed a coat and waistcoat, which I had seen safe about seven o'clock the same evening at the foot of the bed—my house was not locked—my wife was out—I believe this coat and waistcoat are what I missed.
ANN ALLEN . My husband keeps a clothes-shop in Woolwich. At half-past eight o'clock that evening, the prisoner brought this coat and waistcoat, and I bought them of him.
Prisoner's Defence. On the Saturday I was at my uncle's (the prosecutor) and I asked him to lend me 7 s. or 8 s.; he said, "No; but he had a coat and waistcoat at home, that I could go and take when I liked."
RICHARD WARWICKSHIRE re-examined. I told him so a day or two before.
NOT GUILTY .

1385. JAMES CRAWLEY , was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD JOB FRANCIS . I am the son of Rebecca Francis, who keeps the Bull-inn, Shooters'-hill. On the 17th of March, the prisoner came to me for half-a-pint of beer, I served him—he put down a bad 6 d.—I saw it was bad—I cut it in two, and kept one half, and he took the other half away—I am sure he is the man—when he took one half he knocked the other off the counter—he asked for a candle, my mother would not let him have it, and he went away without it—I picked the other half up the next morning, and put it into a china cup, and at last gave it to the policeman—on the 22nd, the prisoner came again for half-a-pint of beer—he put down another bad 6 d.—I showed it to my mother—I told her it was bad—he laid down another sixpence, which was good—I went for a policeman, and gave him into custody, with the sixpence.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did not you pick up one-half of the sixpence you cut, and the prisoner the other? A. No—on the second occasion the prisoner said, "Is it a bad one?"—I said, "Yes," and he paid another.
REBECCA FRANCIS . On the 17th of March the prisoner came—he gave my son a sixpence—it was cut in two, and one half fell down—he wanted a light, and I would not let him have it—on the 22nd he came again—he paid my son a bad sixpence—my son brought it to me—I said it was bad—I cut it with a penknife, sent for a policeman, and gave him the sixpence and the piece.
WILLIAM BECK ( police-constable R 214.) I was sent for, and took the prisoner—I told him it was for uttering a counterfeit sixpence, and said he had been there the week previous—he denied it, and said he came from Maid-stone that day—I received this sixpence and half a sixpence
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. This sixpence is a counterfeit, and this is half of another counterfeit sixpence.
GUILTY . Aged 47.— Confined Six Months.

1386. JAMES DOUGLAS DIXON , was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 purse, value 6 d.; 1 crown-piece; 9 s.; 1 sixpence; 3 d.; and 6 halfpence; the property of Charles Jacobs: to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
